ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
This report was prepared by the European Commission and the Ageing Working Group to deliver on the
mandate the Economic and Financial Affairs Council gave to the Economic Policy Committee (EPC) in
2024 to update the triennial projections of age-related public spending. The work to fulfil this mandate
is performed within the EPC’s Ageing Working Group (AWG).
The Ageing Report provides long-term projections for public expenditure on pensions, health care, longterm care and education in the European Union and Norway. The 2027 Ageing Report is the eighth
edition and covers the period up to 2080. The budgetary projections, which will be published in spring
2027 in a separate report, build on the macroeconomic assumptions and methodologies discussed in
this report.
This report is presented by the EPC and the European Commission (Directorate-General for Economic
and Financial Affairs - DG ECFIN) after discussion and agreement in the AWG. DG ECFIN provided the
analysis and calculations underpinning the report. Eurostat prepared the demographic projections.
The report was prepared under the supervision of Massimo Suardi (Director in DG ECFIN), Andrea
Oliveira (Chair of the EPC), Nicole Rosin (Chair of the AWG) and Angela D’Elia (Head of Unit in DG
ECFIN). The main contributors were Santiago Calvo Ramos, François Courtoy, Ben Deboeck, Miklos
Erdei, Nicola Gagliardi, Boriana Goranova, Adam Levai and Adja Awa Sissoko, in collaboration with the
Members of the AWG (see list below).

GLOSSARY
DEMOGRAPHY
Total fertility rate (TFR)

Average number of children a woman would have over her lifetime if, at
each childbearing age, she experienced the age-specific fertility rate of
the year in question.

Natural replacement rate

Total fertility rate at which a population replaces itself from one
generation to the next in the absence of migration. Around 2.1 live births
for developed countries.

Life expectancy at birth

Average number of years a newborn would live if exposed throughout life
to the age-specific mortality rates of the year in question.

Life expectancy at 65

Average number of remaining years of life of a person aged 65, given
the age-specific mortality rates of the year in question.

Net migration

Immigration minus emigration over a year. Derived as the population
change not explained by births and deaths, thus including statistical
adjustments, e.g. population revisions.

Old-age dependency ratio (OADR)

Population aged 65 and over as a % of the population aged 20-64.

Very old-age dependency ratio

Population aged 80 and over as a % of the population aged 20-64.

Total dependency ratio

Population aged under 20 and aged 65 and over as a % of the
population aged 20-64.

LABOUR MARKET

x

Labour force (active population)

People who are either employed or unemployed.

Participation rate (activity rate)

Labour force of a given age group as a % of the total population of the
same age group.

Employment rate

Employed people of a given age group as a % of the total population of
the same age group.

Unemployment rate

Unemployed people as a % of the labour force of the same age group.

Average labour market exit age

Average age at which people leave the labour force.

Effective retirement age

Age at which people actually retire, as opposed to the statutory
retirement age.

Statutory retirement age (SRA)

Standard age at which people can draw an old-age pension under the
general scheme.

Early retirement age (ERA)

Earliest age at which an old-age or early pension can be drawn, usually
subject to a minimum contribution period and/or a reduced benefit.

Old-age economic dependency ratio

Inactive population aged 65 and over as a % of the employed population
aged 20-64 (or 15-74).

Total economic dependency ratio

Total inactive population (all ages) as a % of the employed population
aged 20-64 (or 15-74).

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
1.

2027 AGEING REPORT: MANDATE, GENERAL PRINCIPLES AND PROCESS

To assess the long-term sustainability of public finances in the EU Member States, the ECOFIN Council
mandated the Economic Policy Committee (EPC) to update its comprehensive long-term budgetary
projections by the summer of 2027. The update of the Ageing Report, a joint report by the EPC and the
European Commission (DG ECFIN), should be based on new population projections by Eurostat. The
report is prepared by the EPC’s Ageing Working Group (AWG). The 2027 Ageing Report is the 8 th edition.
The Ageing Report’s long-term economic and budgetary projections are a unique exercise, covering the
EU Member States and Norway for the period 2025-2080. The report provides for these countries a
vast set of demographic, labour market, growth and spending indicators. These give insight into the
timing of population ageing, its estimated economic implications and the expected budgetary
consequences. Such pressures have become tangible already in many countries and will only grow as
the number of retirees swells because of the large baby boomer cohorts, in combination with the more
lasting features of steady gains in life expectancy and historically low fertility rates.
Being a joint EPC-Commission product, the Ageing Report provides a shared assessment between the
Member States and the Commission on how ageing costs might develop in the future. The projections
are therefore a cornerstone of various surveillance processes at the EU level. They inform the
coordination of economic policies, in particular the European Semester, Member States’ medium-term
fiscal-structural plans and the assessment of the sustainability of public finances.
Overview of the projection exercise
Graph 1 summarises the process for the triennial update of the report. In a first phase, the AWG agrees
on the underlying assumptions and projection methodologies, which are subsequently endorsed by the
EPC. The results of the first phase are summarised in this report. In a second phase, the age-related
spending projections are prepared, which are the subject of a separate report, to be published in spring
2027.
Part I of this report gives an overview of the demographic and macroeconomic assumptions. The
EUROPOP2025 population projections by Eurostat serve as a starting point. In addition, based on
common methodologies, macroeconomic projections are prepared for each country. These cover
economic growth and its drivers, namely changes in labour productivity (total factor productivity and
capital deepening) and in the labour force (participation, employment and unemployment rates), as
well as interest rates and inflation. A statistical annex at the end of the report gives a country-bycountry overview of the demographic and macroeconomic projections. The full dataset with annual
figures is published online.
Part II details the methodologies used to project age-related expenditure in the second phase of the
process. The budgetary projections cover four items: public spending on pensions, health care, longterm care and education. For pensions, Member States prepare projections based on national models,
which are the subject of an in-depth peer review by the Commission and the AWG. This approach
allows capturing the specificity of each country’s public pension system, while ensuring cross-country
consistency since the projections are based on shared assumptions and methodologies. The
Commission prepares the health care, long-term care and education projections based on common
models, the results of which are discussed and agreed within the AWG.

1

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Graph 1:

Overview of the Ageing Report projection exercise

Source: European Commission, EPC.

The Ageing Report’s long-term baseline projections apply a general ‘no-policy-change’ assumption.
Thus, they illustrate what the future might look like if current policies remain unchanged and given a
number of realistic assumptions would come to fruition. Projecting economic and budgetary
developments fifty years into the future involves much uncertainty. Therefore, a series of sensitivity
tests and alternative scenarios is conducted around the baseline to assess the responsiveness of the
projections to changes in the main underlying assumptions. In addition to the common tests covering
demographic and macroeconomic variables (see Table 1), specific scenarios apply for each expenditure
item.

2.

MAIN RESULTS: THE ECONOMIC IMPACT OF POPULATION AGEING

The EU population is projected to start falling in the coming years while the number of older
people grows, in particular when measured relative to the number of people of working age

Relating the 65+ population to the 20-64 year-old population provides the old-age dependency ratio.
This ratio gives an idea about the relative shift between potential retirees and potential workers and
thus of how an ageing population alters the beneficiary-contributor balance. As a result of the
projected dynamics, this ratio will rise sharply in all Member States over the coming decades (see
Table 2). In the EU, it would rise by about 27 pps, from 38% in 2025 to 65% in 2080, with most of the
increase expected already by 2050. Put differently, the EU goes from having 26 people aged between
20 and 64 for every ten people aged 65 or over in 2025, to less than 16 people by 2080.
Projected changes in the size and age profile of the population are determined by assumptions for
fertility rates, mortality rates and migration (see Table 3).
• The total fertility rate in the EU is projected to rise from an historical low of 1.3 live births per
woman in 2024 to 1.5 by 2080. This all-in-all modest recovery reflects convergence, in the very
long run, towards the Member States with the most favourable fertility developments. Fertility rates
would stay below the natural replacement rate of 2.1 in all countries, ranging between 1.3 and 1.7
by 2080.
• Life expectancy at birth for men is expected to increase by 7 years in the EU, from 79 in 2025 to
86 in 2080. Female life expectancy at birth would rise by about 6 years, from 84 in 2025 to 90 in
2080, thus leading to a further convergence between sexes. When looking at the remaining life
expectancy at the age of 65, the average increase is about 5 years for both. These projections are
based on the hypothesis of a partial upward convergence in longevity between Member States.
• Net migration is assumed to decline from the elevated levels of recent years and to stabilise as
of the 2030s at around 1.4 million people annually (0.3% of the EU population), closer to pre-2022
levels. Countries’ long-term immigration and emigration rates are based on a partial convergence
to past trends for the EU as a whole. In addition, in case of a decline in the working-age population,
10% of this decline is assumed to be compensated by additional non-EU immigration, while two
thirds of the displaced Ukrainians benefiting from temporary protection are assumed to
permanently settle in their host country.

3

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Labour force participation is projected to rise, driven by older workers and women in general, but
would only partially compensate for the decline in the working-age population
The macroeconomic implications of the demographic projections depend on how many people take part
in the labour market. The Ageing Report’s labour force projections follow a cohort approach that
captures the current situation in each country. It assumes no further policy changes beyond already
legislated pension reforms. The participation rate of the EU working-age population is projected to
increase by around 3 pps, from 81% to 84%, and by almost 8 pps for people aged 55-64, to about
78% (see Table 4). Participation among people aged 65-69 would nearly double, from 20% to 38%,
with the largest increase expected for those countries that adjust their legal retirement ages for rising
life expectancy. The upward trend reflects the combined effect of pension reforms on the exit
behaviour of older age groups, lifting the average labour market exit age by about two years, and the
progressive increase in the labour market attachment of female cohorts, with participation rising by
about 5 pps among women aged between 20 and 64, against 2 pps among men by 2080.
However, in most countries, higher participation is expected to be insufficient to offset the projected
decline in the working-age population. As a result, the EU labour force is projected to decrease by 17%
(36 million people) between 2025 and 2080, or 0.3% annually. In 11 Member States, the labour force
would shrink by more than a quarter by 2080, while just five countries are projected to have a larger
labour force in 2080 than in 2025.

Employment rates are projected to increase, though the number of employed people falls relative
to the number of inactive people
The employment assumptions are determined by the population, participation and unemployment
projections. The unemployment projections are based on estimates of structural unemployment which
in most cases lead to a decline in unemployment, by around 1 pp for the EU average (see Table 4).
Employment in the EU is projected to increase from around 76% of the working-age population in
2025 to around 80% in 2080. This 4 pps increase includes a 5 pps increase for women and about 2
pps for men, so that by 2080 some 77% of women and 83% of men aged 20-64 would be in
employment. The employment rate of people aged 55-64 is expected to rise by about 8 pps.
While a higher share of the population is expected to be employed, a shrinking working-age population
means that total employment would nevertheless decline. As a result, the old-age economic
dependency ratio would rise from 47% in 2025 to 74% in 2080, with a fast increase in the next few
decades already (see Table 2). This ratio, which measures the number of inactive people above the age
of 65 against employed people aged 20-64 provides a summary indicator of the expected
macroeconomic repercussions of demographic ageing. It means that the EU goes from having 21
employed people aged between 20 and 64 for every ten inactive people aged 65 or over in 2025, to
just 13 people by 2080.

With labour inputs expected to shrink, labour productivity would become the sole growth driver in
most countries, with GDP growth projected at 1% in the EU in 2026-2080
Differently from previous projection rounds, the long-term GDP projections were prepared by the EPC’s
Potential Output Working Group, extending the methodology used for the Commission’s medium-term
potential growth estimates (T+10) to the full projection horizon (T+55). The new methodology was
applied to the Commission 2026 spring forecast.
Real GDP growth is projected at an average of 1% in the EU in the period 2026-2080 (see Table 5),
declining from 1.2% in the second half of the 2020s to 0.9% in the 2070s. On the one hand, the
contribution of labour to GDP growth is expected to turn negative as of the early 2030s, reducing
growth by 0.3 pps on average over the projection period. This decline results from a lower share of
people of working age in a shrinking total population. On the other hand, hourly labour productivity

4

Executive Summary

would grow by 1.3% on average, of which 0.8 pps comes from total factor productivity (TFP) and 0.5
pps from capital deepening.
Beyond growth, the projections rely on common interest rate and inflation assumptions, which matter
for the indexation of pension benefits and for the return on pension assets. Long-term interest rates
converge from country-specific, market-based expectations towards a common value of 2% in real
terms by T+30, while inflation converges to central bank targets by 2030.

3.

COMPARISON WITH THE 2024 AGEING REPORT ASSUMPTIONS

In base year 2025, the EU counted about 1.1 million fewer people than anticipated in EUROPOP2023,
published in the spring of 2023 (see Table 2), reflecting data revisions for Germany, Poland and
Bulgaria. With few exceptions, fertility was weaker than previously assumed (see Table 3). Conversely,
migration turned out higher, with 1 million more net arrivals in 2025 than assumed in the previous
report. The EU population is projected to shrink more by 2070( 1), counting 9.5 million fewer people
compared to the demographic assumptions underpinning the 2024 Ageing Report, though the picture
varies significantly across countries, ranging from a 14% larger population in Cyprus by 2070 to a
13% smaller population in Hungary than previously projected by 2070 (see Table 2). Average fertility is
generally lower since fertility rates have fallen further in recent years in nearly all countries, including
the current best performers (see Table 3). Longevity, though still expected to rise, would do so at a
slower pace than assumed before, with a larger revision for men. Net migration, by contrast, would be
higher, reflecting both stronger recent outturns and the assumption that a larger share of displaced
Ukrainians will stay permanently.
Participation and employment rates being generally higher in 2025 than projected in the 2024 Ageing
Report, the labour market starting position is more favourable (see Table 4). Revisions for the base
year resonate in the projections, with, for most countries, a larger increase in overall participation and
employment rates than previously projected. Revisions in employment are predominantly driven by the
revisions in the population projections compared with EUROPOP2023.
Compared with the 2024 Ageing Report, average GDP growth is 0.2 pps lower in the EU in 2026-2070
(see Table 5), stemming from lower hourly labour productivity. Growth in the number of hours worked
is similar. The largest downward revisions concern Estonia, Italy and Hungary, the largest upward
revisions are for Malta, Cyprus and Poland.

(1)

The comparison is based on 2070, which was the end-year of the 2024 Ageing Report projections.

Executive Summary

9

Part I
Underlying assumptions

1. DEMOGRAPHIC PROJECTIONS
1.1.

INTRODUCTION

Demography – population change – is shaped by three fundamental factors: births (+), deaths (-), and
net migration (+/-). Hence, dynamics in fertility, mortality and migration drive the ageing of societies,
reshaping economic and social structures. This chapter outlines the key demographic assumptions and
projections underpinning the 2027 Ageing Report, based on EUROPOP2025 (2), Eurostat’s latest 2025based population projections published in April 2026 covering the 27 EU Member States and, amongst
others, Norway.
EUROPOP2025 applies a ‘partial convergence’ approach, meaning that the country-specific key
demographic determinants converge in the very long term towards common values. Setting the
convergence point far into the future – even beyond the endpoint of the projections – has the
advantage of taking due account of recent country-specific trends at the start, while assuming that
Member States’ demographic drivers will converge over time. These demographic determinants are the
fertility rate (impacting the number of births), the mortality rate (impacting the number of deaths and
life expectancy) and net migration (impacting the population growth beyond the ‘natural’ growth due to
births and deaths).
The projection methodology assumes that fertility and mortality rates converge to those of the ‘best
performing’ Member States. As a result, fertility rates would rise in all Member States between 2025
and 2080, faster in the countries that currently have the lowest rates. Similarly, life expectancy follows
an upward convergence trajectory, with longevity increasing relatively faster in countries with currently
the lowest levels of life expectancy. Net migration is estimated through separate emigration and
immigration flows, based on past trends, the latest empirical evidence, long-term partial convergence
and intra-EU flows consistency. Moreover, when the working-age population shrinks, additional
‘partially compensating’ immigration is assumed.
1.2.

FERTILITY RATES

Total fertility rate is assumed to rise in all Graph I.1.1:
Member States over the projection horizon
3.0
but to remain persistently low, well below
the natural replacement rate, recovering
from 1.3 to 1.5 on average in the EU.

Total fertility rate, EU, 1960-2080
EUROPOP2025

Total fertility rates (TFR(3)) declined sharply in EU
Member States following the post-war ‘baby
boom’. From an EU average of 2.6 in 1960, the
number of live births per woman declined
steadily in nearly all countries, to less than two
children on average in 1980, thus below the
natural replacement rate of 2.1 (see Table I.1.1).
By the mid-1990s, fertility rates fell below the
natural replacement rate in all Member States.
The total fertility rate in the EU seemed to have
reached its nadir by the turn of the century,
hovering around 1.5 for about two decades (the

Source: Eurostat, see below.

(2)

For the methodology, see Eurostat (2026).

(3)

Fertility rates reflect the average number of children a woman would have, should she, at each childbearing age, have the
fertility rates of the year under review. This number is obtained by summing the fertility rates by age and is called the
Total Fertility Rate or TFR.

average masking temporary increases in a number of Member States) but total fertility rates
decreased again in nearly all Member States in the last 10 years and have eventually decreased to
below 1.5 in all but three Member States. The EU average has plummeted to an all-time low of 1.3 in
2024.

Fertility assumptions for EUROPOP2025 are driven by two hypotheses: first, the current, very low
fertility rates observed in most EU Member States are at least partially due to the deferment of births.
This means that due to individual preferences, as well as uncertain conditions, persons delay having
children. In addition, societal attitudes have shifted, and births at older ages have become more
accepted. While some of the births that have not taken place in recent years may thus take place later,
there is evidence that delaying births generally leads to a lower overall fertility rate, particularly in
countries where mothers’ age at first birth is high. The implications for the EUROPOP2025 round are
that there is an expectation of some recovery of TFR on average, but that in the long run, fertility will
remain relatively low.
The assumptions on future values of total fertility rates (TFR) are based on three components: a
country-specific trend extrapolation covering the time period 2025 and 2026, a return to a mid-term
value (average of last 5 observed years) between 2027 and 2034, and a partial convergence
assumption from 2035 onwards. For EUROPOP2025, the ultimate convergence value – reached only in
2150 – is 1.64 live births per woman, derived from projecting TFR of the EU frontrunners in fertility
development, notably Ireland, Sweden, France, Denmark, Finland, Belgium, and the Netherlands.

1.3.

MORTALITY AND LIFE EXPECTANCY

Following the recovery from the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, life expectancy at birth
is assumed to increase by 7 and 6 years for men and women, respectively, and by around 5
years at the age of 65 for both sexes over the projection horizon for the EU as a whole.

Part I
Underlying assumptions

Life expectancy has been increasing substantially since 1960 in all EU Member States (see Table I.1.3).
The average life expectancy at birth increased by around 12 years in the EU, from around 67 years in
1960 to 79 years in 2024 for men and from 72 to 84 years for women.
The COVID-19 pandemic had a massive impact on mortality leading to drops or stagnation of life
expectancy in all Member States but the upward trend resumed afterwards and life expectancy at birth
has recovered since, with the 2024 level reaching or exceeding the 2019 level in all but one Member
State (the Netherlands). In this context, no specific adjustment to assumptions is made due to the
effect of COVID-19.
Life expectancy at birth, 2019, 2020 & 2024 – impact of COVID-19 pandemic

There is no consensus among demographers on very long-term trends, e.g. whether there is a natural
biological limit to longevity, the impact of future medical breakthroughs or the long-term effect of
public health programmes and societal behaviour such as the reduction of smoking rates or a
higher/lower prevalence of obesity. On the one hand, most demographic projections assume that gains
in life expectancy at birth will slow down compared with historical trends. This is because mortality
rates at younger ages are already very low and future gains in life expectancy would require
improvements in mortality rates at older ages, which statistically have a smaller impact on life
expectancy at birth. On the other hand, the wide range of life expectancies across EU Member States,
also compared with developed non-EU countries, points to considerable scope for future gains.
EUROPOP2025 assumes, from 2025 on, age- and Graph I.1.4:

in previous projection rounds due to the recent
80

and persons below the age of 20 (4).

1.4.

NET MIGRATION

Migration is the most volatile component in population projections, and, at the same time,
for many countries the only driver of population growth. Annual net migration inflows into
the EU are projected to decrease in the next few years from the current high levels and to
stabilise at around 1.4 million people, more comparable to pre-2022 levels (0.3% of the
population).

16

Part I
Underlying assumptions

From 1960 through the mid-1980s, net migration was mostly positive with annual net inflows
averaging around 130 000, though certain years saw large net outflows. Since 1985, annual net
migration has been consistently positive for the EU. Despite high volatility, it rose significantly: annual
net entries averaged 622 000 people in 1990-1999 and around 1.1 million in 2000-2008. Following a
slowdown to around 500 000 people in 2009-2011 in the wake of the global economic crisis, net
migration started to rise again. In 2015 it peaked at 1.4 million due to large inflows because of
instability in North Africa and the Middle East. Net inflows have remained high ever since. After a brief
decrease, they rose again over 1 million in 2018 and increased to all-time highs in the bulk of Member
States, to several millions overall in 2022 mainly due to the inflow of displaced people fleeing
Ukraine (5). Since that historic high, migration flows have slowed down somewhat in the EU, and in
many countries, recent data indicate a reversal of the record high levels towards lower net
migration (6).

(5)

Eurostat online table on decisions granting temporary protection by citizenship, age and sex – annual data (migr_asytpfa).
The 2020-2024 average figure in the table is an underestimation, as the downward revision of German population figures
in 2022 is implicitly included in and thus absorbed by net migration (including statistical adjustment); while net migration
per se was roughly 1.5 million in 2022. They furthermore do not include refugees under temporary protection in Poland
and Slovakia as the data transmitted to Eurostat for population and migration data exclude this group. For the purpose of
population projections, Eurostat has added the stock of beneficiaries of temporary protection on 1 January 2025 to the
jump-off population for Poland and Slovakia.

(6)

Due to the lack of revisions of migration flows data for many Member States, net migration measured as the difference
between the total population stocks on 31 December and 1 January for a given calendar year, minus the natural change
(the difference between births and deaths) is used in population projections as the benchmark for production of migration
assumptions. The population stocks that Member States transmit to Eurostat represent the number of persons with usual
residence in the country for at least 12 months (or legal or registered persons), including refugees and, in some cases,
asylum seekers who are resident in the country for at least 12 months. This method is different from the approach of
subtracting recorded emigration flows from immigration flows. Please note that while the above-described method for the
projections that is based on averages of intercensal periods is the more reliable measure of net migration overall, net
migration figures derived this way for individual years are not. This is for example visible in the year 2022 for Germany
and in 2020 for Poland, where the break in series of population figures due to the 2021 Population and Housing Census
impacts significantly the derived annual net migration.

17

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Compared to fertility and mortality, migration is the most volatile component of population projections.
Immigration and emigration are generally determined by push and pull factors that are not explicitly
modelled in population projections (such as crises, wars, see above). In the future, climate change is
expected to become a prominent driver of demographic changes, specifically of global migration flows.
Over the long horizon, the levels of immigration and emigration are also determined by the age
distribution of the population and the capacity of a population to absorb immigration.

18

In EUROPOP2025, the expected outflows of
refugees from Ukraine which currently benefit
from temporary protection are in addition
modelled explicitly. The assumption is that 67%
of refugees who are currently beneficiaries of
temporary protection will remain permanently in
the host country, while 33% will return. The
return period is modelled over 10 years, such
that the number of returns per year decreases
linearly, until two thirds of the beneficiaries
registered in the EU at the end of 2024 remain.
For the EU, annual net inflows are assumed to
decrease from the still historically high levels
and return closer to pre-2022 levels as of the
2030s, to around 1.4 million people (0.3% of the
EU population). Total net migration in the period
up to 2080 would amount to around 80 million
people in cumulated terms, equivalent to 18% of
the 2025 population.

2,000

projected to increase by 27 pps, from 38% in
1,000

0

because the impact of decreasing fertility rates on births had partly been offset by the impact of
decreasing mortality rates on deaths.

Table I.1.7 presents an overview of the population
projections for the period 2025-2080 (7). These
provide the basis for the age-related expenditure
projections in the 2027 Ageing Report. According to
the baseline demographic projections, the EU
population would reach a peak by the end of this
decade. It is projected to increase by only one million
in the next years to 453 million people in 2029.
Thereafter, the population would start to shrink, to
414 million in 2080. This is a projected decline by
8% compared to the base year level, most of which
would take place in the second half of the projection
period.
The overall downward trend comprises rather
heterogeneous developments at the country level.
While population is projected to decrease in most
Member States, it would stagnate e.g. in France, and
limited growth is projected in some such as Spain. As
a result, the most populous Member States in 2025
(Germany, France, Italy, Spain then Poland) are
projected to remain the same in 2080 but Spain is
projected to have more inhabitants than Italy by the
end of the projection period.

The age structure of the population will change
dramatically in all Member States. The share of the
age groups above 65 years in the overall population is projected to rise (see Table I.1.8), from 22% in
Source: European Commission based on EUROPOP2025 (Eurostat).

(7)

The population projections published by Eurostat refer to the population on 1 January. The projections in this report for
year t are calculated as the average of the Eurostat projections on 1 January for year t and those for year t+1.

19

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

2025 to 33% in 2080 for the EU, i.e. one in three persons is projected to be at least 65 years old at the
end of the projection period. The share of people aged 80 years and above would roughly double or
more in all Member States (increasing from 6% to 14% for the EU as a whole). At the same time, the
population share of the youngest age group (0-19) would shrink in all Member States but Bulgaria
(from 20% to 17% for the EU). Finally, the population of working age (20-64 year-olds), would also
shrink in all Member States relative to the overall population, from 58% in 2025 to 51% in 2080 for
the EU as a whole.

Drivers are similar to the above-mentioned trends. First, the increasing weight of older age groups is
due to the combination of large cohorts born in the 1950s and 1960s and continued gains in life

20

Part I
Underlying assumptions

expectancy. Second, the size of the working-age population shrinks significantly between 2025 and
2080 (see Graph I.1.8) due to the low fertility rates and a shrinking number of women in childbearing
ages. Finally, net migration flows would not suffice to offset the trends towards an ageing population.
The strong upward shift in the age distribution over the next decades can also be seen on the
population pyramid (Graph I.1.8). While in 2025 the largest age groups for both men and women were
people aged 50-59, in 2080 the 60-69 age groups would be the largest for both sexes. Overall, the
median age is projected to rise from 45 to 51 years in 2080 (see Graph I.1.9).

Due to the demographic shift from younger to older age groups, demographic dependency ratios are
projected to increase significantly in all countries (see Table I.1.9). The old-age dependency ratio
(OADR), i.e. the number of people aged at least 65 divided by those aged 20-64, gives an idea about
the relative shift between potential retirees and potential workers and thus of how an ageing
population alters the beneficiary-contributor balance (8). The OADR is projected to increase from 38%
in 2025 to 65% in 2080 in the EU. This increase would predominantly take place during the first half
of the projection period. The change in the OADR means that the EU would move from having, for
every person aged over 65 years, nearly three (2.6) people of working age to less than two (1.6).
Similarly, the very old-age dependency ratio (people aged 80 or above relative to those aged 20-64) is
projected to rise, from 11% to 28% on average in the EU. Finally, the total dependency ratio (the sum
of people younger than 20 and older than 64 years relative to the population aged 20-64) is projected
to rise from 72% to 98% for the EU. This measure relates the theoretical inactive population – people
that have not yet entered or have already left the labour market – to the theoretical contributory base.

(8)

Economic dependency ratios are discussed in Chapter 2.

21

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

1.6.

COMPARISON WITH THE DEMOGRAPHIC PROJECTIONS IN THE 2024 AGEING REPORT

The EUROPOP2025 projects a sharper decline in the EU population (9.5 million people less
by 2070) than assumed in the 2024 Ageing Report. The impact of lower fertility rates is only
partly offset by higher net migration assumptions. A greater decline is projected for the
working age population and especially for the youngest age group that is only marginally
compensated by a slightly higher projection for the older age group. As a result, the old-age
dependency ratios based on the new demographic projections show an even more dramatic
increase than in the 2024 Ageing Report.

(9)

22

The starting point of the demographic projections
based on EUROPOP2025 is lower by a million people in
2025 than in the 2024 Ageing Report (EUROPOP2023),
mainly due to the inclusion of revised population
figures for a number of countries, notably Germany (9).
This is mainly reflected in fewer young people at the
start of the projection, then population decreases
faster in the working age group and even more so in
the youngest age group resulting in 9.5 million fewer
people by 2070, which was the end-year of the
previous report (see Table I.1.11). These changes result
in a slightly lower projected old-age dependency ratio
in the beginning but higher levels by the end of the
projection period (see Table I.1.10).
The main driver of these differences in the population
projections is the lower assumption on fertility rates
that plummeted in recent years. Even if recovery is
assumed, figures are lower throughout the projection
horizon as, in addition to the lower starting point, the
convergence value became lower, too, since the decline
in fertility rates was encompassing (including “best
performers”). Assumptions on life expectancy are lower
across the board, with a slower increase in longevity.
Finally, net migration is assumed to be higher than in
the 2024 Ageing Report. A main source of that
difference is due to higher outturn data for the most
recent years, showing higher net migration even
beyond the shock caused by Russia’s prolonged
aggression against Ukraine as well as different
assumptions on the outflow of refugees from Ukraine,
namely a twice as high proportion assumed to remain
(see Table I.1.12).

Revised levels following the 2021 Population and Housing Census have been included for all countries in EUROPOP2025.

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Box I.1.1: Demographic ageing in a global context

The EU's share of the world population is projected to shrink further, from 6% in 2020 to 4% by
2080. The projected increase in dependency ratios is comparatively high for the EU, in particular
given the current demographic balance compared to ‘younger’ continents.
The UN population statistics and projections provide a global perspective of demographic
trends (1). The combined share of EU Member States in the world population halved since 1960,
when the EU27 represented almost 12% of the world population (see Table 1). While the EU
population grew by 25% over six decades, demographic growth was even (much) faster in the
rest of the world, with the global population increasing by more than 150% over the same period.
The shares of China, Japan, Russia and the US in the global population also declined compared to
1960, in contrast with the rising shares of India, Latin America and, particularly, Africa.
Given that fast population growth is expected to continue, the African continent’s share in the
world population would increase further, to about a third of the world population by 2080. While
staying the most populous continent, Asia’s share would decline over the decades to around 50%.
This fall is driven by India, Japan and, in particular, China. By 2080, the EU’s share in the global
population is projected to reach 3.6%, shrinking by 2 pps relative to the current situation (2). This
is comparable to the projected share of the US, whose share would remain broadly stable.

Global ageing shows in the projected changes of median ages. In 2020, the median age in the EU
was 44 years, more than 10 years higher than 50 years ago. This compares to 38 years in
Northern America, 31 years in Asia and 30 years in Latin America, as well as worldwide, with just
19 years in Africa. By 2080, half of the EU population would be above 51 years according to
EUROPOP2025, compared to 47 years in Latin America, 44 years in Northern America, 45 years
in Asia and 40 for the world, based on the UN projections.
Looking at the age structure in the UN projections, the EU currently has already a comparatively
high old-age dependency ratio (OADR). At 35%, it is below the Japanese ratio of 53% but
considerably above that in other large countries, with a higher increase in recent decades. Ratios
and projected changes for the EU are comparable to those based on the Eurostat projections (see
Table 2). Globally, the UN demographic projections show an increase in the OADR by 24 pps, from
(1) The latest projections are the UN World Population Prospects 2024.
(2) The UN projections and Eurostat’s EUROPOP2025 projections differ notably due to the different methodologies. The
former expects the EU population to decrease over the entire projection horizon to 370 million people in 2080, i.e.
44 million below the Eurostat baseline.

16% in 2020 to 40% in 2080. The EU ratio would increase by close to 30 pps, reaching almost
65% in 2080. All continents are expected to see an increase and, in some cases, e.g. Asia and
Latin America, the projected change in the balance between potential retirees and potential
contributors is similar to or even somewhat worse than what is anticipated for the EU. However,
given that the current situation is generally more favourable in those other regions, it can be
concluded that EU Member States will on average undergo a more radical ageing process than
the rest of the world, notable exceptions such as Japan and China aside. Looking at developments
for the very old-age dependency ratio leads to broadly the same conclusion.

25

2. LABOUR FORCE PROJECTIONS
Labour force participation among the population aged 20 to 64 years in the EU is projected
to rise from around 81% in 2025 to 84% in 2080. Higher participation of older workers and
of women in general are the main factors behind this projected trend. The labour supply,
however, is projected to shrink by 17% over the same period, as higher labour force
participation would only partly offset the steep projected fall in the working-age
population. The employment rate in the EU is projected to increase from 76% in 2025 to
80% in 2080. Revisions versus the 2024 Ageing Report in employment are driven by the
updated demographic projections.
2.1.

INTRODUCTION

The macroeconomic repercussions of the demographic trends described in the previous chapter will
depend in large part on future labour force developments, namely how many people take part in the
labour market and for how long. More people working and doing so on average for a longer period
broadens the contributory basis, generating more resources to finance the rising pension, health care
and long-term care costs that are generally associated with population ageing, without crowding out
other government spending.
How long people will work in the future depends, among other factors, on the incentives created by
public and private pension schemes. It is therefore important to consider the likely impact of adopted
pension reforms on labour market exit behaviour (10).
This chapter starts with a comparison of recent trends in labour force participation, followed by an
overview of the estimated effects of legislated pension reforms. Thereafter, the participation rate and
employment rate projections are presented (11). An analysis of the economic dependency ratio and a
comparison with the 2024 Ageing Report conclude the chapter. The boxes and annexes focus on the
underlying assumptions and provide a detailed overview of the methodology.
2.2.

PAST TRENDS AND MAIN DRIVERS OF LABOUR MARKET DEVELOPMENTS

The composition of the labour force has changed fundamentally in recent decades. Younger cohorts
have tended to enter the labour market later, while women and older people have steadily increased
labour market participation. These dynamics have several drivers:
• Demographic factors, e.g. a delay in childbearing and a decline in fertility rates.
• Sociocultural factors, e.g. longer schooling and the fading of the breadwinner model.
• Institutional factors, e.g. increases in legal retirement ages and stricter labour market legislation.
• Economic factors, e.g. more part-time employment and a shift to a service-based economy.
Labour market participation has been trending upwards. Among all EU people of working age (aged 2064) it rose from 74% in 2005 to 77% in 2015 and to 81% in 2025 (see Table I.2.1). Compared to
2015, participation rose in all EU Member States, with the largest increases for Hungary and Malta, at
about 10-15 pps, followed by Poland with an 8 pps increase. Intra-EU differences have been narrowing
but are still substantial with a difference of almost 17 pps in 2025 (down from 23 pps in 2005)

26

(10)

Other aspects that may affect labour supply are health and disability conditions, as well as active labour market policies
to increase demand for older workers and the flexibility of working in old age.

(11)

To project participation rates by sex and single age, the Cohort Simulation Model (CSM) developed by the European
Commission (DG ECFIN) is used (see Box I.2.1 and Annex 1).

Part I
Underlying assumptions

between the Member States with the highest and lowest participation rates among people aged
between 20 and 64.
Rising female labour force participation has been the predominant driver behind rising overall
participation. Among men, labour participation in the EU was stable at around 82% in the 2000s but
has been rising slowly since. Given the fast rise in women’s participation, the sex gap has narrowed
(from almost 17 pps in 2005 to about 9 pps in 2025). Yet, a considerable gap remains, also because
male participation has picked up. Even though women’s participation has been steadily increasing, it
stays below male participation for all age groups in nearly all countries. In general:
• Prime-age men (aged 25-54) have the highest participation rates of all age groups, at over 90% in
nearly all countries.
• The participation rates of men aged 55 to 64 years rose considerably in the past twenty years,
given a general increase in statutory retirement ages and tightened access to early exit pathways.
For women aged 55-64, the increase was even stronger, though starting from a lower level,
reflecting higher female participation at lower ages and a convergence in retirement conditions
with those for men in most countries.
• Compared with 2005, participation rates of young people (aged 20-24) have declined in a majority
of countries as people tend to study longer. This trend has reversed over the past decade, though,
with higher participation in most countries in 2025 compared with 2015.
• Given these ongoing trends, a further increase in total labour market participation can be expected,
thanks to prime-age women and older workers in general.

2.3.

PENSION REFORMS AND OLDER WORKERS’ LABOUR MARKET PARTICIPATION

Tighter eligibility criteria and stronger retirement disincentives will induce older workers to
stay longer in the workforce and delay retirement. As a result, participation among older
age groups is projected to rise strongly in many countries and the age at which people exit
the labour market is expected to increase by about two years on average in the EU by 2080.
Pension reforms in EU Member States
The vast majority of Member States has legislated pension reforms in recent decades. Some countries
enacted systemic reforms, which revise the fundamental design of the public pension scheme, for
example when moving from the traditional defined benefit scheme to a point system or notional
defined contribution scheme. Most changes have been parametric, though: altering certain settings
without touching on the overall framework. Two broad types of parametric interventions can be
distinguished: altering the eligibility requirements such as age and contributory period or changing
benefits’ generosity by adapting the pension formula (e.g. accrual rate, pensionable earnings,
valorisation of accrued rights) and indexation rules. Some countries also increased the contributions for
current workers to strengthen the internal financial balance of their pension systems or created
pension reserve funds.
Statutory retirement ages (SRA)

Among eligibility measures, the most frequent reform consists in raising the statutory and early
retirement ages. It is the measure with the largest direct impact on labour market exit behaviour as it
forces people to delay retirement. Nearly all Member States have already increased their early and
statutory retirement ages or are in the process of doing so in the coming years (see Annex 3 - Table
II.A3.2). As a result, the average statutory retirement age for men (women) is expected to rise from
65.5 years (64.8) in 2025 to 66.6 years (66.4) in 2050, and to 67.6 years (67.4) in 2080 (see Graph
I.2.1), with often similar increases in the early retirement age.
Some countries have introduced an automatic indexation of legal retirement ages to longevity to make
their pension system more robust to the expected continuous increase in life expectancy (see Table
I.2.4). It forces people to delay retirement. The adoption of an automatic balancing mechanism within
the pension system or a sustainability factor, which instead corrects the pension benefit for longevity,
are other types of adjustment mechanisms found in some Member States. While they contribute to the
financial sustainability of pension schemes, they have at most an indirect impact on labour market
participation.
Box I.2.2 at the end of the chapter provides an overview of ongoing reforms with an expected impact
on effective retirement ages in the 27 Member States and Norway. These reforms are incorporated in
the Cohort Simulation Model which the AWG uses to project participation rates in the context of the

• In Slovakia, the 2019 reform capped the statutory retirement age at 64 years, lower for mothers,
thus abolishing the link to life expectancy adopted in 2012. However, in 2023, the link to longevity
was reintroduced.
• Spain suspended the application of the index for pension revaluation and the sustainability factor
and eventually repealed both mechanisms in 2021. Instead, a ‘closure clause’ (cláusula de cierre)
was introduced, which could trigger an automatic increase in social security contributions if pension
spending net of revenue measures is projected to exceed a specific threshold. This mechanism,
which upon its first evaluation was not activated, is not accounted for in the projections.
• The Netherlands introduced a full adjustment of its statutory retirement age to longevity in 2012,
to be enacted as of 2024. The 2019 reform lessened the link to 2/3rd of the gain in life expectancy.
• In Bulgaria, the 2015 reform stipulates that, once the statutory retirement ages for men and
women will have equalised at 65 years in 2037, they should be automatically extended in line with
changes in life expectancy. The precise way such link would be enacted has not been legislated,
though. As a result, the link is not included in the Ageing Report’s pension projections for Bulgaria.

Reform-induced changes in participation rates and exit ages
The age group 55-64 is the most affected by measures aimed at postponing retirement. Table I.2.1
showed how average participation rate for this age bracket in the EU rose from 43% in 2005 to around
70% in 2025. Graph I.2.2 shows the estimated impact of pension reforms by 2080. Reforms adopted
across Member States should lift participation among men aged 55-64 years by about 6 pps on
average by 2080. Among women the projected increase is 7 pps on average. Also for the age group
65-69, legislated reforms are expected to lift participation (see Graph I.2.3), with an average increase
of about 17 pps for both men and women by 2080, though mainly in countries that adjust their legal
retirement ages to life expectancy.

2.4.

LABOUR MARKET PARTICIPATION AND LABOUR SUPPLY PROJECTIONS

Social and institutional factors such as a higher attachment to the labour market of
younger women and adopted pension reforms are expected to push up participation rates.
This increase somewhat offsets the impact of a decrease in the working-age population on
the labour supply.

2.4.1. Participation rate projections
The total participation rate in the EU is projected to increase by around 3 pps by 2080, with
women’s labour market participation anticipated to rise by 5 pps compared with 2 pps for
men, and large increases (8 pps on average) among the older labour market segments.
As discussed in Chapter 1, the population of working age is projected to decline substantially in the
coming decades as large cohorts of retiring people are replaced by smaller cohorts of younger workers.
Other things being equal and given the age profile of participation rates, the increasing share of older
workers in the labour force pulls down the total participation rate.
The projections nevertheless reveal a rightward shift in the age profile of both male and female
participation, particularly at ages over 60 years (see Graph I.2.5). For female participation, there is in
addition a general upward shift. These broad trends reflect the combined effect of pension reforms
and the rising attachment of younger generations of women to the labour market.
Tables I.2.5, I.2.6 and I.2.7 provide an overview of the main projected developments for the
participation rates, broken down by broad age group and sex. In the EU, the labour market participation
of those of working age (20-64y) would increase by about 3 pps between 2025 and 2080: from 81%
to 84%. This increase is dominated by rising participation of women, with a change of close to 5 pps
compared with less than 2 pps for men. With 81% of the female working-age population expected to
be active in the labour market in 2080, women’s participation would nevertheless remain 6 pps below
that of men, compared with 9 pps at present.

Participation among people aged 20 to 64 is expected to rise in all countries. The largest increases are
projected for Italy, Denmark, Slovenia, Ireland, the Netherlands and Estonia, with participation projected
to rise by between 4 and 7 pps by 2080. Estonia, the Netherlands, Denmark and Sweden are the
countries with the highest projected participation rate in 2080, at above 90%. This compares with rates
that remain below 80% for Romania, Italy, Belgium and Luxembourg by 2080.
When comparing projections for male and female participation for the 20-64 age group, aside from
Romania, all countries show a higher increase for women. Despite the resulting convergence, overall
participation of women would remain below that of men throughout the projection period in all
Member States except Estonia and Lithuania, the only countries where female participation would
exceed 90%.
Participation among the youngest age bracket (20-24y) is expected to rise in all countries, by around
2 pps on average. Differences between countries and between sexes remain substantial for this age
group considering the assumptions made in the Cohort Simulation Model for this age group (see
Box I.2.1).
For the prime-age group (25-54y) an average increase of close to 2 pps is projected in the EU, with a
maximum rise of around 4 pps for Denmark, the Netherlands, Ireland and Estonia. Decreases of less

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

than 1 pp are projected for Greece, Spain and Romania. By 2080, only Italy and Romania would have
prime-age participation rates below 85%, while twenty Member States would have a participation rate
over 90% among people aged 25-54. Male participation in the 25-54 age bracket is expected to
remain broadly stable at an elevated level, with the EU average participation rate rising slightly to
around 93% and projected changes limited to ±2.5 pps in all countries. In contrast, female participation
is projected to rise by 3 pps on average among the same age group, with an increase in all countries
aside from Czechia, Greece, Spain and Romania. The average difference in participation for prime-age
men and women would still amount to 6 pps in 2080, compared with 9 pps in 2025. Only in Estonia
and Lithuania, women aged 25 to 54 years are projected to become more active than men of the
same age.
Participation among the group of older workers (55-64y) is expected to increase in all countries, often
substantially. The EU average rises by almost 8 pps by 2080, to around 78%. The projected increase
exceeds 15 pps for Italy, Slovenia and Slovakia. Participation would remain below 70% by 2080 in
Belgium, Croatia, Luxembourg, Poland and Romania. With higher increases for women (+10 pps) than
for men (+5 pps), the gender gap is projected to narrow substantially and even to be closed for several
countries, which is currently already the case for Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania and Finland. By 2080 this
would also be the case for Malta and Slovakia.
As Graph I.2.5 showed, the increase in labour market participation extends beyond the age of 64, also
considering the envisaged rise in legal retirement ages in many countries. Indeed, when considering the
65-69 age group, there is a similar upward trend in participation (see Graph I.2.6). From 20% in 2025,
the average participation rate rises to 38% in 2080, though with large differences between Member
States. The largest increases occur for countries with a link to life expectancy, with participation
projected to rise by 25 pps or more in Denmark, Greece, Italy, Cyprus, Portugal and Slovakia.

2.4.2. Breakdown of changes in participation rates
Table I.2.8 applies a shift-share analysis to the change in the participation rate by 2080 along age and
sex dimension. The overall change in the participation rate is broken down in a pure participation rate
effect, a demographic effect and a residual interaction effect.

35

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

The participation rate effect captures changes in participation rates of specific age and gender groups.
It is positive for all Member States. This reflects the trend increase in the participation rates of women
and older workers, with a small decrease in participation among prime-aged people in some countries.
The demographic effect, capturing changes in the structure of the working-age population, is negative
in most Member States because of shrinking cohorts of prime-aged people and women in general.
Women are thus associated with both positive participation effects and negative demographic effects.
The former reflects the upward transmission of younger cohorts’ higher participation rates, as captured
by the CSM. The latter reflects the ageing of the population, with a stronger impact on women, mostly
because they leave the labour force, on average, earlier than men.

2.4.3. Projection of labour supply
The total labour supply in the EU is expected to decrease by 17% over the projection period,
with a decrease of at least 25% in many Member States.

Graph I.2.7 highlights the substantial differences
in labour supply projections across Member
States, ranging from an increase by 17% in
Luxembourg(12) to a decrease by more than a
third in Greece, Poland, Latvia and Lithuania in

7% on average in 2025-2050 is followed by

2050 the labour force would still grow in several
countries, during the remainder of the projection
period, it is expected to decline in all countries
and those with the sharpest fall in 2025-2050
also show the steepest decline in 2050-2080.

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

2.5.

EMPLOYMENT PROJECTIONS

Employment in the EU is projected to increase from around 76% of the working-age
population in 2025 to around 80% in 2080. This change mostly reflects higher employment
rates among older people and women in general, given higher participation among these
groups and the assumption of declining unemployment over time.
Employment is calculated as a residual variable, being determined by the EUROPOP demographic
projections (see Chapter 1), the participation rates projected through the CSM (see Section 2.4) and the
unemployment rate assumptions (see Box I.2.3).
Graph I.2.8:

Age profile of employment rate by sex, EU, 2025 & 2080

women 2025

Source: European Commission, EPC.

The employment rate among people aged between 20 and 64 in the EU is projected to rise from
around 76% in 2025 to 80% in 2080 (see Table I.2.10). This overall increase in the employment rate
by 4 pps results from a 5 pps increase in the female employment rate and a smaller increase of about
2 pps in the male one (see Tables I.2.11 and I.2.12). The age profile in Graph I.2.8 tracks that of
participation, with a rightward shift for both men and women and higher employment for women at
most ages by 2080. The unemployment assumptions, with a general decline in the unemployment rate
from current levels in the next decades (see Box I.2.3), contribute to the general increase in
employment rates. Despite the strong increase in women’s employment, a higher share of the male
population aged 20-64 would nevertheless be employed in 2080: 83% compared to 77% for women.
Employment among older workers is expected to rise considerably over the projection period for both
sexes, with increases of 5 pps and 10 pps in the respective employment rates of men and women aged
55-64. This reflects how recent pension and labour market reforms in many Member States incentivise
older workers to retire later. By 2080, the employment rate for people aged 55-64 would remain below
65% in Luxembourg, Poland and Romania, which currently are among the countries with the lowest
participation for the age bracket, in particular among women, and, aside from Romania, have no
planned increase in the legal retirement ages (see Table II.A3.2 in Annex 3).

2.6.

ECONOMIC DEPENDENCY RATIOS

The old-age economic dependency ratio (inactive elderly versus employed people) is
projected to increase significantly in all Member States, especially over the next several
decades. Similarly, the total economic dependency ratio (inactive versus employed people) is
expected to rise but less so. Variability across countries is large, though.
The old-age economic dependency ratio provides a summary indicator of the expected macroeconomic
repercussions of demographic ageing. The indicator expresses the inactive elderly population (65+) as
a share of total employment (aged 20-64 or 15-74). This ratio would rise sharply: from 47% in 2025
to 64% in 2050 and 74% in 2080 (from 44% to 60% and 69% in terms of employment in the 15-74
age group) (see Table I.2.13). This means that, in 2080, for every 3 inactive persons above the age of
65, there would be 4 employed persons, down from more than 6 in 2025.
Across EU Member States, the projected old-age economic dependency ratio for 2080 ranges from a
minimum of 56% in Denmark to a maximum of 90% in Italy. For most countries, the increase at the
start of the projection period, i.e. 2025-2050, is larger than the increase in the period 2050-2080. The
largest total increase is projected for Lithuania, Luxembourg, Cyprus, Ireland, Spain and Poland.
The total economic dependency ratio, calculated as the ratio between the total inactive population,
regardless of age, and those in employment (aged 20-64 or 15-74), measures the average number of
individuals each employed person ‘supports’ economically. Under this broad definition, the dependency
ratio is expected to increase continuously over the projection period, from an average of 114% in 2025
to 133% in 2080 for the EU average (see Table I.2.14). Compared with the old-age economic
dependency ratio, the increase is smaller since it also accounts for the decline in the youngest age
groups in the demographic projections.
The projected development of this indicator reflects the profound societal impact of changes in life
expectancy and fertility rates over the next decades. There are, however, large differences between
countries. The increase ranges from more than 35 pps in Spain and Luxembourg, to just 2 pps in
Denmark, where it would even decline initially, as is the case for Malta, Finland and Sweden.

2.7.

COMPARISON WITH THE LABOUR MARKET PROJECTIONS IN THE 2024 AGEING REPORT

Labour market indicators for base year 2025 were generally better than assumed in the
2024 Ageing Report. The improved starting point reverberates in the updated labour market
assumptions, though overall revisions in employment are mostly driven by the revisions in
the population projections compared with the previous update.
This section provides a comparison of the main labour

offset by a higher participation rate and a similar
unemployment rate. Looking at individual countries,
revisions in the population projections, in both
directions, are the principal driver behind changes in
the employment figures.

For twelve countries the 2070 employment figure is lower than under the 2024 Ageing Report
projections, with the largest revisions for Hungary, Estonia, Sweden, Bulgaria and Italy, which are also
the countries with the biggest downward revisions in working-age population for 2070. The sixteen
countries with higher projected employment by 2070 all saw the working-age population revised up
(aside from Slovenia), with higher participation further pushing up the employment projections (aside
from Finland). The largest upward revisions in the employment projections are seen for Lithuania,
Latvia, Cyprus, Ireland and Spain. Graph I.2.9 visualises the high correlation between revisions in the
employment and population projections.

The labour force identity L = E + U can be written as E = P x PR x (1-UR) where L
is the labour force, E is employment, U is unemployment, P is the population, PR
is the participation rate, and UR is the unemployment rate. Taking the logarithm
of the above expression, revisions in employment level can be approximated as
follows:

Part I
Underlying assumptions

Box I.2.1: Cohort Simulation Model (CSM)

The cohort simulation model (CSM) as developed by the European Commission (DG ECFIN) (1) has been used
since the 2006 Ageing Report to project participation rates by sex and single age. The rationale for using
the CSM is to reflect the substantial changes in labour market behaviour in recent decades across different
cohorts and sexes. This methodology accounts, for instance, for the significant rise in female labour force
participation in recent decades, as younger women, with a much stronger attachment to the labour force,
gradually replace older women with relatively low participation rates. Simultaneously, the cohort
methodology also caters for a – generally small – decline in the participation rate of men in recent
generations in several countries, opposite to the trend observed for women.
The central feature of the CSM is the estimation of labour market exit and entry rates for a ‘synthetic’
cohort (2). Because no individual longitudinal data on labour market transitions is available, participation
data by single age is used to estimate labour market transitions, namely whether people either entered or
exited the labour market between two consecutive ages. These probabilities are calculated on the basis of
changes in the participation rate for the past ten years − 2016-2025 for the 2027 Ageing Report. This
reference period provides thus a ‘synthetic’ cohort for base year 2025. A 10-year average is used to avoid
that the choice of a particular year unduly influences the calculation of entry and exit rates through cyclical
fluctuations or statistical errors.
Graph 1: The Cohort Simulation Model

The entry and exit rates are then used to project future participation rates as older generations are
progressively replaced by younger ones. The projections rely on a no-policy-change assumption meaning
that for Member States which legislated pension reforms, average exit rates for the age group 51-74 are
adjusted to account for the reform impact. Otherwise, rates are kept constant throughout the projection
period. By keeping rates constant, the cohort effect is captured: in the long term, we arrive at a population
with the same individual characteristics as the cohorts who entered the labour force most recently, as
measured through the 10-year average.
The 2027 Ageing Report projections build on the latest Eurostat demographic projections (see Chapter 1),
prepared independently by Eurostat with consultation of National Statistical Institutes. Together with the
participation rate projections, the population projections determine the labour force assumptions.
The following assumptions are made:
•

Base year for the labour market projections is 2025, the projection period covers 2026-2080.

•

Average entry/exit rates are calculated as a ten-year average (2016-2025), using participation rates
by sex and single age (15-74) from the harmonised EU Labour Force Survey of Member States as
compiled by Eurostat.

•

A corrective mechanism for young cohorts is applied to avoid that any increase in education enrolment
rates (and the corresponding decline in participation rates) feeds into future declines of participation
rates for prime-age workers. This assumption means that participation rates at each single year of age

(1) The methodology was initially developed at the OECD, see Burniaux et al. (2003).
(2) A more detailed description of the methodology can be found in Annex 1.

(Continued on the next page)

45

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Box (continued)

between 15 and 19 remain constant at the 10-year average. Participation rates between ages 20 and
29 are allowed to increase if this is the outcome of the model. Otherwise, rates are kept constant.
•

46

Pension reforms are modelled through their estimated impact on the labour market exit rates of older
workers (aged 51-74). This is based on a best-reasoned judgment, using the probabilistic nature of the
CSM. Specifically, the historical average exit rates of older workers, calculated separately for both sexes,
are adjusted to account for the expected effects of enacted pension reforms. The estimation of the
adjustment considers country-specific information about the relationship between retirement behaviour
and the parameters of the pension system, as well as cross-country evidence. This framework for
analysis is able to incorporate a broad typology of measures, inter alia, increases in the statutory or
early retirement age, the convergence of lower female statutory retirement ages to that of men,
changes in financial incentives for early or delayed retirement and the linking of the statutory
retirement age to changes in life expectancy. For the latter mechanism, the assumed increase in the
effective retirement age, as approximated through the average labour market exit age, equals about
three quarters of the projected increase in the statutory retirement age.

Part I
Underlying assumptions

Box I.2.2: Pension measures incorporated in the labour force projections
This box lists the most recent pension reforms
legislated by the Member States that are
considered to have a direct impact on the labour
market exit behaviour of the oldest workers and
that are thus relevant for the participation rates as
projected through the CSM model. This box
describes the situation on 30 September 2026.
The countries with the most significant new
measures since the 2024 Ageing Report are
Belgium, Czechia, Romania, Slovenia and Norway,
all of which have legislated higher retirement ages
or tighter conditions for early exit.

The preferential career break for certain civil
servants, which resulted in a faster build-up of
career years, is being phased out and harmonised
at a lower level for teachers and police officers,
among others. The statutory retirement age for
railway drivers and the military is being aligned
with the general regime: from 55 years with a 30year career to 67 years in 2038 for railway drivers,
and from 56 years to 67 years in 2048 for the
military. As of 2027, calculation of civil servants’
pensions is based on an extended working period
instead of the last ten career years, such that the
full career is taken into account by 2062.

Belgium

Bulgaria

The statutory retirement age in the three main
public old-age pension schemes (wage earners,
self-employed and civil servants) rose from 65
years to 66 years in 2025 and will reach 67 years
in 2030. A career of 45 years remains required for
drawing a full pension.

Following the 2015 reform, the statutory
retirement age is rising to 65 years: by 2029 for
men and by 2037 for women (63 years in 2029).
After 2037, the statutory retirement age is
supposed to increase in line with gains in life
expectancy, though no clear rule has been
legislated. Therefore, such mechanism is not
included in the projections. The retirement age for
workers in the defence and security sector is
gradually increasing by two months annually, to 55
years in 2029.

Early retirement is possible at the age of 63 with a
career of 42 years, at 61 with a 43-year career and
at 60 with a 44-year career. As of 2027, a career
year is counted only if 156 days were effectively
worked or assimilated (104 days before), and most
periods of unemployment and of career
interruption at the end of the career are excluded.
As of 2027, it also becomes possible to retire at
the age of 60 with 42 contribution years, provided
that each of these years comprises at least 234
effectively worked or assimilated days.
A new bonus/malus system was adopted in 2026
and applies as of 2027. It is based on an effective
employment condition: people with fewer than 35
years of at least 156 effectively worked or
assimilated days (and 7020 worked days in total)
lose 5% of their benefit for each year of
anticipation with respect to the statutory
retirement age. The malus is phased in by cohort:
2% per year for people born up to 1965, 4% per
year for those born in 1966-1974 and 5% per year
for those born as of 1975. In addition, assimilated
periods can account for at most 40% of the total
career as of 2027, falling to 20% in 2031.
The same career condition applies to the pension
bonus: people who have accumulated 35 years
with at least 156 effectively worked or assimilated
days receive 2% for each extra year of work if born
before 1963, 4% for the 1963-1972 cohorts and
5% if born as of 1973.

The required career length for workers in normal
work conditions to qualify for retirement is rising
by two months annually, until it reaches 40 years
for men and 37 years for women in 2027.
People within 12 months of the statutory
retirement age can retire early, in which case the
benefit is reduced by 0.4% for each month of
anticipation. Early retirement is also possible for
people working under hazardous and unhealthy
working conditions or special groups such as
teachers, military and police officers (55-60y).
Czechia

Statutory retirement ages are determined in
function of a person’s birth year and, in the case of
women, the number of children raised. For men and
women born in 1960, the statutory retirement age
is 64 years and 2 months (63y8m, 62y2m, 60y8m
and 59y2m for women with 1, 2, 3-4 and more
than 4 children respectively). It rises to 65 years for
people born in 1965, i.e. in 2030 (64y8m, 63y2m
and 61y8m for women with 2, 3-4 and more than
4 children), and to 65 years and 8 months for all
people born in 1973, i.e. in 2038, regardless of the
number of children. Thereafter, the statutory

it reaches 67 years in 2056.

and replaced a similar senior disability pension.

The minimum insurance period is 35 years or 30
contributory years. Without this minimum
insurance period, retirement is possible only two

previously).

Germany

Early retirement is possible three years prior to the
statutory retirement age, with a minimum
insurance period of 40 years (35 years before the
2023 reform). The insurance period includes noncontributory periods. With at least 45 insurance
years under a strict definition, the penalty for early
retirement is 0.75% per 90 days or 3% per year
(6% before); with fewer than 45 years, the penalty
is 1.5% per 90 days or 6% per year.
Denmark

The standard retirement age reached 67 years in
2022. The statutory retirement age and the VERP
age (voluntary early retirement pension with
minimum 30 contributory years) increase based on
life expectancy for a 60-year-old – the unweighted
average for men and women is used – with the
period on old-age pension targeted at 14.5 years
(17.5 years including VERP).
Adjustments to life expectancy must be confirmed
by Parliament 15 years before they take effect (12
years for changes in the VERP age), with further
updates every five years. Increases to 68 years in
2030, to 69 years in 2035 and to 70 years in 2040
were confirmed in 2015, 2020 and 2025
respectively; the 22 May 2025 Law sets the
retirement age at 70 years for all people born after
1970. The maximum increase in the retirement age
is limited to one year for every update. In the
projections, it is assumed that Parliament confirms
the increases in the retirement age beyond 2040
given the established track record.
In 2020, a new early retirement scheme was
created for people with long careers: people with
44 years of labour market seniority (at the age of
61 years) obtain the right to retire 3 years before
the standard pension age. People with respectively
42 years and 43 years of labour market seniority
can retire 1 and 2 years early.
In 2019, a new right to disability pension was
created for people 6 years or less removed from
the statutory retirement age, on the conditions of
at least 20-25 years of labour activity and a
reduction in the ability to work to a maximum of

As a result of the 2007 reform, the statutory
retirement age is gradually increasing, reaching 67
years in 2031. Early retirement is possible (i) at the
age of 63 for people with an insurance record of at
least 35 years, with a permanent reduction of 0.3%
for each month below the statutory retirement age;
(ii) without a deduction for people with a
contribution history of 45 years, at the age of 64
years and 6 months in 2025, rising to 65 years by
2029.
Since 2023, it is possible to combine an early
pension with additional earnings without any loss
of pension entitlements. Since 2026, the Aktivrente
scheme allows people who have reached the
statutory retirement age to earn up to EUR 2 000
per month tax-free through work.
Estonia

The retirement age for men and women was
equalised in 2016 at 63 years and reached 65
years in 2026. The 2018 reform introduced a link
to changes in life expectancy at the age of 65,
applied as of 2027 and taking into account the
change over five years. The maximum annual
increase is three months; any increase in excess of
three months is carried over to the next
adjustment. The change in the retirement age is
known two years in advance.
The old bonus/malus system was replaced by an
actuarially neutral system in 2021. The early
retirement age (previously three years before the
statutory retirement age) is 1/2/3/4/5 year(s) lower,
for contribution periods of 20/25/30/35/40 years.
Drawing a pension benefit can be combined with
work.
Ireland

Ireland does not have a statutory retirement age.
Retirement ages are generally determined by the
terms of an individual's employment contract. The
State Pension Age in Ireland is 66. There is no
requirement for an individual to retire from
employment in order to receive a State Pension
(Contributory). Eligible employees may notify their
employer that they do not consent to retire at the
contractual retirement age.
