State Pension Deferral was introduced in 2024.
Individuals who turn 66 may defer claiming their
State Pension (Contributory) until age 70 in return
for an actuarially adjusted higher weekly pension
payment. Individuals aged 66 to 70 who defer
claiming their State Pension (Contributory)
continue to pay social insurance contributions.
These contributions may be used to establish
entitlement to, or increase the rate of, a future
State Pension (Contributory).
Greece

The 2012 and 2014 reforms increased the
statutory retirement age from 65 to 67 years (with
at least 15 contribution years) and the early
retirement age from 60 to 62 years (with 40 years
of service, a penalty applying otherwise) for the
whole social security system. There are minor
exceptions for private sector employees who had
already 25 years of service by 2010, who can retire
before the age of 62. Since 2021, the minimum
and statutory retirement ages are adjusted in line
with changes in life expectancy at the age of 65.
Upon its first implementation in 2021, the integer
year(s) of the change over the period 2010-2020
was(were) to be taken into account. Thereafter, the
integer year of the change in life expectancy is to
be re-examined every three years.
The 2021 adjustment was cancelled because the
COVID-19 pandemic had caused a drop in life
expectancy at 65. In 2024, the change in life
expectancy at 65 relative to 2010 was less than
one year, so the mechanism was not activated,
keeping the retirement ages at 67 and 62 years.

63 years in 2027 with a contributory period of at

contribution years. Below 33 contribution years
early partial retirement is not possible.
Early retirement is penalised in function of the
distance to the statutory retirement age and the
contribution period. People working beyond their
statutory retirement age increase their benefits by
4% per extra year of work, regardless of the
contribution history. People can opt for a lump sum
payment or a mixed pay-out.
France

People can retire from the moment they reach the
earliest retirement age, with a penalty if they do
not meet the required contribution period. They can
also delay their retirement to obtain a full-rate
pension which is granted to people with the
required contribution period or have reached the
age for a full-rate pension of 67 years.
Following the 2023 reform, the earliest retirement
age for most schemes rises gradually from 62 to
64 years. In addition, the required insurance period
rises faster to 43 years than under the previous
reform timetable. In the public sector, for some
special branches with ‘active service’ (e.g.
policemen and nurses), the earliest retirement age
is 57 years, rising to 59 years following the 2023
reform.

The statutory retirement age is rising to 67 years
in 2027 and the contributory career for a full
pension to 37 years. Workers with a contributory
period of at least 38.5 years can retire at the age
of 65 with a full pension.

For pensions taking effect as of 1 September 2026,
this calendar has been suspended until January
2028. As a result, the 1964-1968 cohorts are
allowed to retire one quarter earlier than foreseen.
People born in 1969 or later remain subject to the
earliest retirement age of 64 years. The required
contribution period for a full-rate pension is also
reduced by one quarter, but only for the 1964 and
1965 cohorts.

Early retirement for voluntary retirees requires a
minimum age of 65 (as of 2027), a minimum
contributory period of 35 years and the resulting
benefit should be at least the minimum pension.

Certain groups can receive a full-rate pension
before the automatic full-rate pension age of 67.
People recognised as disabled can retire at full rate
from age 62.

Early retirement for involuntary retirees (collective
dismissals) requires a minimum retirement age of
63 years (as of 2027) and a minimum contributory
period of 33 years.

Early retirement for long careers is available to
people who started working before the ages of 16,
18, 20 or 21 and meet the required contributory
period, with eligibility conditions depending on the
birth cohort.

Early partial retirement, for 25% to 75% of working
time, is available for workers of different ages

Croatia

The statutory retirement age is 65 years for men
(64 years for women in 2026, rising to 65 years in
2030), with 41 insurance years needed for a full
pension. Early retirement is possible at the age of
60 (59 years for women in 2026, rising to 60 years
in 2030) with a minimum contributory period of 35
years (34 years for women in 2026, rising to 35
years in 2030). Penalisation of 0.2% per month of
anticipation, i.e. maximum 12%, which since 2026
is no longer applied once the age of 70 is reached.
Bonus of 0.45% per month beyond the statutory
retirement age since 2023 (0.34% before).
Bonus in case of long career (41 years): 0.15% per
month until the statutory retirement age is reached
and 0.45% per month thereafter. Persons working
in arduous or hazardous occupations and those
covered by special schemes, such as military and
police personnel, may qualify for earlier retirement
and more favourable pension conditions. For these
categories, insurance periods are calculated in
extended duration (each 12 months of work
counting as 14, 15, 16 or 18 months), and the
statutory retirement age may be reduced
depending on the degree of increment of the
insurance periods and the length of such periods
Italy

Since the latest positive update to life expectancy,
which took effect in 2019, the statutory retirement
age is 67 years for men and women in all sectors
until 2026, with a minimum of 20 contributory
years.
Irrespective of age, women can retire with 41 years
and 10 months of contributions and men with 42
years and 10 months until 2026 (early retirement).
For ‘precocious workers’ (workers with at least 12
months of contributions from actual work before
the age of 19) who are in special conditions
(unemployed, caregivers, workers with a certified
reduction in work capacity of at least 74%, and
those engaged in heavy labour) retirement is
permitted with only 41 years of contribution until
2026.
Under the standard early retirement regime, for
workers enrolled after 1995, whose benefits are
therefore calculated entirely under the Notional
Defined Contribution (NDC) system, early
retirement is also permitted at 64 years, i.e. three
years before the statutory retirement age, if they
have at least 20 years of effective contributions,

with both requirements applying until 2026 and
subject to life-expectancy adjustments thereafter,
provided that their expected pension is not below a
minimum threshold: three times the old-age social
allowance for men and for women without children,
rising to 3.2 times as of 2030, and 2.8 and 2.6
times the social allowance for women with one
child and with two or more children respectively.
For all three early retirement pathways described
above, pension payments start 3 months after the
eligibility requirements are met. For ordinary early
retirement and precocious workers, progressively
longer waiting periods apply to certain categories
of public employees, reaching 9 months from
2028.
Eligibility requirements for old-age pensions, early
retirement pensions and the social allowance are
updated every two years to reflect changes in life
expectancy at 65. The updates for 2021, 2023 and
2025 did not result in an increase.
The 2026 Budget Law re-determined, for 2027, an
increase in the access requirements of one month
and confirmed the three-month increase as of 1
January 2028. Employees engaged in strenuous or
burdensome activities are exempted from the
increases in access requirements for 2027 and
2028. For military personnel retiring under lowerthan-general requirements, eligibility requirements
will increase by an additional month in 2028, a
further month in 2029 and another month in 2030,
on top of the general adjustment to changes in life
expectancy.
Workers may receive an allowance (‘APE Sociale’,
classified as social assistance expenditure and not
included in pension expenditure) to bridge the period
before meeting the old-age pension requirements. It
is available to workers in special circumstances –
the unemployed, caregivers, workers with a certified
reduction in work capacity of at least 74% and those
engaged in heavy labour – who are aged 63 years
and 5 months and have accrued at least (i) 30 years
of contributions or (ii) 36 years of contributions in
an arduous job (32 years in the construction sector).
The scheme has been extended for 2025 and 2026.
The temporary ‘Quota 103’ scheme (a minimum
age of 62 years and 41 years of contributions, with
full application of NDC rules) was extended for
people eligible in 2025 but has not been renewed
for 2026. It followed the temporary schemes
‘Quota 100’ (2019-2021: 62 years and 38 years of
contributions) and ‘Quota 102’ (2022: 64 years and
38 years).

‘Opzione donna’ allows women aged 61 years (60
and 59 years with one child and with two or more
children respectively, or 59 years regardless of the
number of children for women affected by
company crises) with 35 years of contributions
under special conditions (caregivers, women with a

subject to benefits being entirely computed

Cyprus

The 2012 pension reform introduced a link
between the statutory retirement age and changes
in life expectancy at the statutory retirement age,
with an automatic adjustment every five years. The
first revision, calculated in 2024 and applied in
2025, covered the change in life expectancy over
2018-2023 compared with the 2013-2017 base
period. It had no impact and the statutory
retirement age remained at 65 years. The next
revision is scheduled for 2030.
Early retirement is available at the age of 63
provided that certain contribution requirements are
met, including a minimum of 780 weeks of insurance and at least 70% of the required insurance
points. A permanent penalty of 12% for early
retirement is under discussion, as part of a reform
expected by 2027. Miners and specific professions
may retire earlier under special provisions.

(15y) but less than the mandatory contribution

Early retirement is possible as of five years prior to
the statutory retirement age, on the conditions of
a minimum of 30 years of service and not receiving
any other social insurance, state or social
assistance pension. Early old-age pensions are
calculated in the same way as regular old-age

of 0.32% (maximum of 19.2%) for each full month
remaining until the statutory retirement age.
After reaching the statutory retirement age, a
person may continue working while receiving an
old-age pension. If retirement is deferred, the
pension is permanently increased by 8% for each
additional year of deferral.
Luxembourg

To be eligible for an old-age pension, an insured
person needs to be at least 65 years old and have
accumulated a total of 10 years of contributory
periods. An insured person that is at least 60 years
old and has accumulated a combined total of 40
years of contributory periods (work) and credited
non-contributory periods (e.g. years of study or
years spent raising children) qualifies for early
retirement, provided that contributions have been
paid for at least 10 years. Since July 2026, 40
years and 1 month are required, rising further over
2027-2030 to 40 years and 8 months. Individuals
that are at least 57 years old gain access to early
old-age benefits if they accumulated a total of 40
years of compulsory insurance periods (work).

Latvia

As a result of the 2012 pension reform, the
retirement age increased by three months every
year, reaching 65 years in 2025, with a minimum
contributory period of 20 years (15 years until
then). The early retirement age – with an insurance
record of at least 30 years – is two years lower
than the statutory one and thus reached 63 years
in 2025. The early retirement benefit is 50% of the
normal benefit. The full pension is restored upon
reaching the statutory retirement age.
Lithuania

Since 2026, an employee who is eligible for an
early old-age pension at the age of 57 or 60 and
has agreed with their employer to reduce working
hours by at least 25% has the right to a progressive
pension, i.e. the part of the pension corresponding
to the reduction in working time. A similar option
already existed under the special pension schemes
for the public sector.
Since 2026, taxpayers who are eligible for an early
old-age pension but do not exercise that right
benefit from an annual income tax allowance of up
to EUR 9 000.

The statutory retirement age reached 65 years for
both sexes in 2026, with a minimum contribution
period of 15 years. 35 years are needed for a full

The statutory retirement age has been 65 years
since 2022 for both men and women. There is no
early retirement scheme, though women can retire
with 40 years of eligibility period, i.e. contribution

Malta

The 2006 reform entails a gradual increase in the
statutory retirement age, from 61 and 60 years for
men and women respectively to 65 years by 2027
(for people born as of 1962). It stands at 64 years
in 2025 and at 64 years and 6 months in 2026.
The minimum contributory period is 12 years, while
the period required for a full pension increases to
40 years in 2027, to 41 years in 2034 and to 42
years in 2041.
Early retirement is possible as of the age of 61
provided that the retiree will not remain in any
gainful employment and has the required
contribution history: 35 years for those born up to
1961, 40 years for the 1962-1968 cohorts, 41
years for the 1969-1975 cohorts and 42 years for
the 1976 cohort and younger. People who delay
claiming their retirement pension and continue
working see their entitlements increase by about
6.5% to 8% per year of deferral, reaching up to
around 29% for longer deferrals.
The Netherlands

The statutory retirement age reached 67 years in
2024. Since 2025 it is linked to two thirds of the
rise in the remaining life expectancy at 65, instead
of the full link envisaged before. The statutory
retirement age is set five years in advance: an
increase to 67 years and 3 months in 2028 has
been confirmed, and in November 2025 the age
was fixed at that same level up to and including
2031. The duration of social security arrangements
for people below the retirement age (disability
pensions, survivors’ pensions, unemployment
schemes and social assistance) is extended in line
with the increase in the statutory retirement age.
There is no early retirement scheme in the
Netherlands.
Austria

The statutory retirement age is 65 years for men
and all civil servants, with a minimum contribution
period of 15 years. The female statutory retirement
age is rising gradually from 60 to 65 years over
2024-2033, by six months each year; it stands at

As of 2022, the early retirement possibilities
without deductions in case of 540 contribution
months that existed in 2020 and 2021, have been
abolished again for all pension types.
Since 2026, partial retirement, for 25% to 75% of
working time, is possible for people who are eligible
for early retirement and continue to work parttime. A bonus of 5.1% per year, for a maximum of
three years, applies when retirement is postponed
beyond the statutory retirement age.
Poland

The retirement age is 65 years for men and 60
years for women. No minimum contribution
requirements apply. There is no early retirement
possibility, except for special schemes such as
miners and teachers, who can retire up to 5 years
early. No penalty or bonus applies.
Portugal

Under the general contributory regime, an insured
person is entitled to an old-age pension when
having reached the statutory retirement age and
completed the required qualifying period of 15
years with earnings registration. Since 2015, the
statutory retirement age changes in line with two
thirds of the change in life expectancy at the age

of 65. It stands at 66 years and 9 months in 2026
and rises to 66 years and 11 months in 2027. The
statutory retirement age is reduced by four months
for each contributory year above 40 years, with a
minimum retirement age of 65 years.
Early retirement is possible from the age of 60 with
40 contributory years. These conditions are not
adjusted for gains in life expectancy. Two
permanent corrections apply. First, a penalty of
0.5% per month of anticipated retirement, which is
always applied unless the person has 48

amounts to 17.63% in 2026.
Romania

Old-age retirement is possible when the standard
retirement age is reached (65 years for men; 62
years and 6 months for women in 2026, rising to
63 years by 2030 and to 65 years by 2035)
together with a minimum contribution period of 15
years; 35 years are required for a full pension, for
women by 2030. For every child born, the standard
retirement age of women is reduced by six months.
For ‘service pensions’ (army, police, intelligence and
penitentiary administration staff), which covered
4.5% of total pensioners in 2025, the eligibility
criteria are an age of 60 years with at least 25
years of contribution, of which at least 15 years in
service. This age is rising to 65 years over 20302035.
Early retirement is possible up to five years before
the standard retirement age, with at least 35
contribution years. Penalties of 0.2% to 0.4% per
month apply for contribution periods between 35
and 40 years, i.e. a maximum penalty of 24% when
retiring five years in advance with 35 contribution
years and of 12% when retiring five years in
advance with 40 contribution years. The penalty
applies until the standard retirement age is
reached.
After 2035, retirement ages are adjusted for half
of the gains in life expectancy, at intervals of no
more than three years. The projections assume that
the mechanism starts in 2038.

Slovenia

The right to an old-age pension depends on two
parameters that both need to be met: the age of
the insured person (65 years for both men and
women in 2026) and the pension qualifying period
(minimum 15 years). With 40 years of paid
contributions – pensionable service excluding
purchased periods – old-age retirement is possible
at the age of 60 in 2026 without any reduction of
the benefit. This is not considered early retirement
but an old-age pension with a lowered retirement
age, reflecting an earlier entry into the labour
market. In 2026, early retirement is also possible
at the age of 60, with 40 years of contributions
including purchased periods and periods of
voluntary pension insurance after 2013. The
penalty for early retirement is 0.3% for each month
of retirement before the age of 65, i.e. maximum
18%.
Under the 2025 reform, the early and statutory
retirement ages rise by three months annually as
of 2028, until they reach 62 and 67 years
respectively in 2035. The earliest retirement age is
reduced for periods of childcare, compulsory
military service, employment between the ages of
15 and 18, and arduous labour with a detriment to
health.
People that have accumulated 40 contributory
years but continue to work full time can earn a
bonus of 3% per year for a maximum of three
years. If they meet the conditions for old-age
retirement and continue working full time, they are
entitled to 40% of their old-age pension for the
first three years and to 20% thereafter. If they
work part time, they receive part of the accrued
pension benefit (minimum 12.5%, 50% in case of
half-time work and 75% in case of working 25% of
full time). People can also work part time prior to
retirement.
Slovakia

The statutory retirement age for people born up to
1966, i.e. until 2030, is defined by a fixed table
based on cohort, sex and number of raised children
(for women, the retirement age is reduced by six
months for each of the first three children). It
increases by two months for each subsequent
cohort, reaching 64 years in 2030, with 41
contribution years required.
The 2023 reform restored the link between the
retirement age and average life expectancy from
2030 onwards (for people born as of 1967), which

had been abandoned in the 2019 reform, thereby

expectancy and adds this median to the unadjusted
general retirement age of the previous cohort. The

Under the standard early retirement scheme,
people are allowed to retire two years before
reaching the statutory retirement age if the benefit
is at least the subsistence minimum. In that case,
their old-age pension is reduced by 0.5% per
month. The pension benefit is increased by 0.5%
for every additional month of work beyond the
retirement age.
In addition, early retirement is possible with a
career of 40 years, which is also linked to life
expectancy, provided the benefit amounts to at
least 1.6 times the subsistence minimum. A penalty
applies of 0.5% for every month below the
statutory retirement age.
Finland

The lowest old-age retirement age in the earningsrelated scheme rose by three months for each age
cohort, from 63 years in 2018 to 65 years in 2026.
The upper age limit will be raised to 69 years for
those born in 1958-1961 and to 70 years for those
born in 1962 or later. Partial old-age retirement,
for 25% or 50% of the pension, is possible three
years in advance, i.e. at 62 years as of 2026.
The lowest old-age retirement age will be linked to
life expectancy as of 2030 (the 1965 cohort) so
that the time spent working in relation to the time
spent in retirement remains at the 2025 level. The
annual increase of the retirement age is capped at
two months.

Sweden

The retirement age is flexible and individuals can
claim benefits as of a minimum age, 64 years since
2026, without any upper limit.
The 2022 reform increased the earliest age for an
old-age pension from 62 years to 63 years in 2023
and to 64 years in 2026. The earliest age for a
guarantee pension was raised from 65 years to 66
years in 2023 and to 67 years in 2026. The upper
age for employment protection was increased to
68 years in 2023 and to 69 years in 2026.
Thereafter, all age limits are linked to an ‘indicative
age’ which increases in line with two thirds of the
change in life expectancy at the age of 65, i.e. by
one year every time the expected remaining life
expectancy at 65 increases by 1.5 years. When the
indicative age increases by a full year, all age limits
are automatically increased by one year. Applying
the formula to the EUROPOP2025 projection, the
age limits increase by one year in 2042, 2061 and
2079.
Norway

The statutory retirement age stands at 67 years
but serves rather as a reference age since flexible
retirement is possible from the age of 62. To
receive a full guarantee pension, one must have
been a member of the National Insurance Scheme
for 40 years, otherwise the benefit is reduced
accordingly. The guarantee part of the pension can
be drawn only from the age of 67.
Under the 2024 reform, age limits rise in line with
life expectancy from the 1964 cohort onwards, i.e.
as of 2031. The unconditional retirement age for
the old-age pension, currently 67 years, increases
by two thirds of the increase in life expectancy; all
age limits in the pension system are adjusted in
line with it.

Part I
Underlying assumptions

Box I.2.3: Long-term unemployment assumptions

In line with the assumption that the output gap closes in T+5 (see Box I.3.1), actual unemployment rates
are assumed to converge to their structural levels by 2030.
Structural unemployment refers to the ‘natural’ rate of unemployment at which the economy is estimated
to settle in the medium run, in the absence of shocks (i.e. by T+5 in this methodology). Since structural
unemployment cannot be observed, it is typically estimated via methods that pin down its statistical and
theoretical properties. Its level is usually determined by institutional and fiscal factors, such as
unemployment benefits or tax rates, which influence the minimum wage needed for a worker to accept a
job (the ‘reservation wage’).
To proxy structural unemployment, the Ageing Report uses estimates of the ‘non-accelerating wage rate of
unemployment’ (NAWRU). The NAWRU is defined as the unemployment rate consistent with stable wage
growth (1). As labour productivity changes over time (see Chapter 3), the NAWRU is not constant. Rather, it
changes in response to demographic and productivity developments. Moreover, it captures the gradual
elimination of the long-term effects of cyclical unemployment, like hysteresis phenomena (2). Concretely,
the methodology assumes that, in the long term, the NAWRU converges either to the country-specific
‘NAWRU anchors’ or to their EU average, whichever is lower (3).
Anchor values are country-specific estimates derived from a panel regression model in which the NAWRU
depends on a set of cyclical variables (total factor productivity, the long-term real interest rate, a measure
of construction activity, and a measure of industrial confidence), together with a set of structural variables
(unemployment benefit net replacement rates, expenditure on active labour market policies, the degree of
union density, the labour tax wedge, and a demographic index) (4). To derive medium- and long-term
country-specific anchors, non-structural variables are set at their average values, thereby averaging out
the impact of cyclical fluctuations. Structural variables are assumed to remain unchanged at their last
observed values, in line with the ‘no policy change’ principle. At the same time, the demographic index
follows EUROPOP projections, while total factor productivity is set in line with the long-term projections
(see Box I.3.1).
Capping country-specific NAWRU values to the EU average avoids extrapolating excessively high
unemployment rates into the far future. This assumption reflects the idea that some convergence within
EU Member States will materialise over time. Implicitly, the methodology assumes the implementation of
labour market policy measures. For those countries that currently register high unemployment, this is a
crucial assumption, as it implies higher employment and higher GDP growth rates over the projection period.
To ensure consistency between total/average structural unemployment rates and the structure of the labour
force, age-gender specific unemployment rates are calculated as follows for each projection year:

Economic Policy Committee (EPC-POWG), see Arévalo et al. (2026), Planas and Rossi (2020), Hristov and Roeger
(2020), Orlandi et al. (2018), Hristov et al. (2017) and Havik et al. (2014).
(2) Hysteresis is the idea that shocks on the unemployment rate that are initially temporary can have more persistent
effects, for instance through skills erosion as workers stay out of work for longer.
(3) As a general rule, convergence to the country-specific anchors is achieved by T+10 (2035). However, for countries
whose unemployment rate is far from the anchor, convergence may take longer (see Arévalo et al. 2026). Moreover,
in countries where the NAWRU anchor is above the EU average, structural unemployment starts to converge to the
EU average once the country-specific anchors have been reached. In other countries, the NAWRU follows its structural
anchor but, if it is increasing, it is capped to the EU average.

3. GDP PROJECTIONS
3.1.

INTRODUCTION

To project potential GDP growth over the long term, a production function framework is employed. It is
based on the standard specification of the Cobb-Douglas production function with constant returns to
scale. Within this framework, potential GDP growth is driven by long-term developments in labour input
and labour productivity.
Projections of labour productivity are based on assumptions regarding the long-run development of its
underlying determinants, namely labour-augmenting total factor productivity (TFP) and the capital
stock per worker (also referred to as capital deepening). While allowing for cross-country differences in
the short to medium term, these projections assume partial convergence across EU Member States by
the end of the projection period. Labour input projections are based on Eurostat’s latest population
projections (see Chapter 1) and the labour market participation rates projected by the Commission’s
Cohort Simulation Model (see Chapter 2).
Differently from the practice used since the 2015 Ageing Report, the long-term projections of potential
GDP were not directly elaborated by the AWG. Instead, they were produced by the EPC’s Potential
Output Working Group (POWG), based on an extension of the T+10 EUCAM methodology to cover the
period up until T+55 (2080) (13). A description of the production function framework and the key
assumptions underpinning the long-term GDP projections presented in this section are summarised in
Box I.3.1. A more comprehensive account is available in Annex 2. The long-term projections in this
report are based on the Commission 2026 spring forecast. For Norway, ‘mainland GDP’ is used, which
excludes ocean transport and petroleum extraction.
The following sections present the long-term potential GDP projections (Section 3.2) and the main
differences with those of the 2024 Ageing Report (Section 3.3).

1.0

The growth rate of the labour component –
the number of total hours worked – in the
EU is projected to be negative as of the
early 2030s. The demographic assumptions
result in a decline in the working-age
population and, by extension, a negative
contribution of labour input to potential
growth for most EU countries. The projected
increase in participation and employment
rates would not be sufficient to
counterbalance the decline in the workingage population (see Chapter 2). Overall, Source: European Commission, EPC.
over the period 2026-2080, total hours
worked would fall in the EU by 0.3% on average (see Table I.3.2). As a result, potential GDP growth in
the EU would be entirely driven by labour productivity.
Annual growth in labour productivity per hour worked is projected to increase in the first half of the
projections, going from 0.9% on average over the period 2026-2030 to 1.4% over the period 20412060. Thereafter, the annual growth in labour productivity is projected to slow down slightly, reaching
an average of 1.3% over the period 2061-2080. As a result, the average annual growth rate over the
entire projection period (2026-2080) would be 1.3% (see Table I.3.3).
Total factor productivity (TFP) growth would explain around two-thirds of labour productivity growth
during the projection period. For the EU as a whole, the average annual growth of TFP would steadily
increase, from 0.6% in the late 2020s, until around 2050, when it would stabilise at 0.9%. Over the
whole projection period (2026-2080), the growth rate of TFP would average 0.8% (see Table I.3.4).
The contribution of capital deepening to labour productivity would remain about constant at around
0.4%-0.5% throughout the projection period for the EU (see Table I.3.5). For many countries whose
GDP per capita is below the EU average, the capital deepening contribution is projected to be higher
than the EU average, reflecting to some extent the catching-up process on the accumulation of capital.

A summary of the relative contribution to potential GDP growth of labour productivity and labour input
(and their determinants) over the entire projection period 2026-2080 is provided by the standard
growth accounting framework reported in Table I.3.6.
For the EU, a broadly stable decline in total hours worked is projected over the projection period.
Employment rates are projected to increase, making a small positive contribution to potential growth
(0.1 pp). However, this would be more than offset by the decline in total population and the share of

59

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

the working-age population, which would put a negative drag on potential growth (-0.2 pp each).
Consequently, on average, labour input would contribute negatively to annual potential output growth
(by 0.3 pps in the EU).
By contrast, growth in labour productivity (production per hour worked) would contribute positively to
potential output growth in the EU, i.e. 1.3 pps on average in 2026-2080. Overall, the annual potential
GDP growth over projection period 2026-2080 would average 1% in the EU.
The contribution of labour input (total hours worked) to potential growth rates is projected to be
negative for the majority of EU Member States. This mostly follows from the adverse impact of
demographic developments. However, labour input growth would be positive, on average, for some
countries (Denmark, Ireland, Luxembourg, Malta, the Netherlands, Sweden and Norway), because of a
growing population or higher employment rates (see Chapters 1 and 2). The contribution of labour
productivity would be positive for all Member States. Yet, the size of the contribution would differ
substantially across countries, especially in the first half of the projection period, reflecting different
catching-up potential both in terms of TFP and capital accumulation (see description in Box I.3.1).

Actual GDP growth rates are computed by considering the position of the economies in the business
cycle (see Box I.3.1). Specifically, for those countries whose potential GDP is expected to be higher than

growth is projected to be higher than potential GDP growth until the output gap is closed in 2030 (see
Graph I.3.1). The reverse happens for those countries whose potential GDP is projected to be lower than
actual GDP in 2027, i.e. the output gap is positive.

Part I
Underlying assumptions

Box I.3.1: The production function used for long-run growth projections
The long-run GDP projections are based on the Potential Output Working Group’s (POWG) EU’s commonly
agreed methodology (EUCAM) for projecting output up to T+55 (1). This box outlines the rationale and main
assumptions of the methodology, while Annex 2 provides additional technical details.
General framework

EUCAM is based on a standard production function, in which output depends on total factor productivity
(TFP), capital (K) and labour (L). It assumes that output is produced according to a Cobb-Douglas technology
with constant returns to scale. Potential output results from the same production technology, using potential
(or trend) equivalents of the input series:

𝑌𝑡 = 𝑇𝐹𝑃𝑡 ∙ 𝐾𝑡1−𝛼 ∙ 𝐿𝛼𝑡
Within this production function framework, EUCAM projects TFP, capital and labour by applying distinct
methods for different projection periods:
•

Over the short run (up to T+5), EUCAM uses a mix of filtering techniques to decompose actual
GDP, and its underlying input variables, into a cyclical component and a trend component.

•

Over the medium term (from T+6 to T+10), EUCAM applies stabilising rules to ensure smooth
transitions and to reflect the persistence of structural developments.

•

Over the long term (from T+11 to T+55), EUCAM focuses on longer term trends through further
stabilisation and convergence rules.

Actual and potential labour series (L) result from the combination of four factors: working-age population,
participation rate, hours worked per worker and unemployment rate. For potential labour input, the trend or
potential values of these components are used where relevant. Chapters 1 and 2 describe the behaviour of
these factors over the projection period. Therefore, this box does not discuss them further. Instead, it
focuses on the assumptions underlying the evolution of productivity (TFP) and of the capital stock (K).
Total factor productivity (TFP)

In the case of TFP, the trend-cycle decomposition is based on a Kalman filter.
Over the medium term, EUCAM assumes that the growth rate of TFP follows an autoregressive process
with a drift, implying a mean reversion. In practice, this allows for a partial recovery of TFP that
compensates for the declining trend observed in recent years. This is justified on the basis that EU
economies needed time to adjust following recent large economic shocks.
This recovery is, however, assumed to be short-lived. Indeed, theoretical and empirical evidence point to a
persistent slowdown in TFP growth. Relevant evidence includes the secular decline in TFP growth (e.g.
Gordon, 2015), the likely negative TFP effects of the EU’s rapidly ageing labour force (e.g. Aiyar, Ebeke and
Shao, 2016) and climate change (e.g. Cárdenas Rodríguez et al., 2023; Parker, 2023), as well as evidence
that ideas are becoming harder to find (see Bloom et al., 2020). Taken together, these arguments do not
support the view that TFP growth will return to its historical long-run average, despite the potential impact
of AI.
Therefore, over the long term, the projection of trend TFP turns to a more structural model anchored on a
knowledge production function, following Jones (1995, 2005). More specifically, EUCAM assumes that past
discoveries facilitate the generation of new ideas, such that R&D investment has a proportional impact on
(1) The methodology extends the T+10 projections up to T+55, thus covering the entire Ageing Report projection period.
For a detailed description of the methodology, see Arévalo et al. (2026).

(Continued on the next page)

63

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Box (continued)

the stock of knowledge and productivity. Consequently, constant TFP growth is assumed in the long run.
Operationally, from T+11 onwards, TFP is assumed to grow at the same rate as between T+9 and T+10.
In addition, consistently with the evidence of convergence clubs within the EU, the growth rate of TFP of all
Member States is assumed to converge asymptotically to the EU average but at differentiated speeds.
Specifically, convergence is assumed to be completed by T+30 for countries whose TFP growth is below
the EU average in T+10, and by T+55 for countries whose TFP growth is above it.
Capital stock (K)

Because the capital stock measures the economy’s productive capacity, EUCAM does not distinguish
between actual and potential capital. There is thus no need to perform a trend-cycle decomposition (2).
Over the medium and long term, EUCAM assumes that the capital stock evolves according to the standard
law of motion of capital:

𝐾𝑡 = (1 − 𝛿) ∙ 𝐾𝑡−1 + 𝐼𝑡
where δ is the rate of depreciation of capital (3), and 𝐼𝑡 is the gross fixed capital formation.
EUCAM distinguishes two groups of countries to project investment.
For 13 Member States (Bulgaria, Czechia, Estonia, Croatia, Cyprus, Latvia, Lithuania, Hungary, Malta, Poland,
Romania, Slovenia and Slovakia), EUCAM projects investment using an autoregressive process throughout
the projection period (the ‘investment rule’). This approach extends the trends observed in the historical
data and in the Commission forecast.
For the remaining 14 Member States (Belgium, Denmark, Germany, Ireland, Greece, Spain, France, Italy,
Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Austria, Portugal, Finland, and Sweden), EUCAM applies this methodology
only up to T+5. Beyond that, investment projections are modified so that the capital-to-potential GDP ratio
gradually converges to a constant level by T+15 (this is referred to as the ‘transition rule’). From that point,
investment is calibrated to ensure that the growth rate of capital equals the growth rate of potential GDP
(this is known as the ‘capital rule’).
Safeguard clause and closure rule

Once all input series are generated, final GDP values are calculated using the Cobb-Douglas production
function mentioned above. However, this does not fully determine the projection path for those countries
that meet the following two conditions: (i) their ratio of GDP per person of working age is below the EU
average in T+10, and (ii) the average growth rate of this ratio, computed over the period from T+10 to
T+55, is lower than the corresponding EU average. For these countries, the GDP growth projections are
increased so that their average growth rate of GDP per person of working age matches that of the EU,
thereby ensuring that further divergence does not occur in the long run (i.e. the safeguard clause). To
preserve the growth accounting breakdown between GDP and the production inputs, the additional growth
of potential GDP is integrally assigned to the TFP component.
Finally, the closure rule generates the actual GDP growth series by linking the short-term forecast to the
medium-term path of potential GDP through a gradual elimination of the output gap. Up to T+2, actual GDP
follows observed data and the Commission forecast, which differ from potential because of cyclical
conditions. From T+3 onwards, the remaining gap between actual and potential GDP is reduced linearly, so
that by T+5 the gap is fully closed, and actual GDP coincides with potential GDP. This approach ensures
that temporary deviations are phased out smoothly, producing an actual GDP growth profile that converges
in a consistent and economically coherent way towards medium-term potential growth.

(2) Variations in capital utilisation are captured through the cyclical component of total factor productivity.
(3) In the projections, the rate of depreciation is fixed to its last forecast value, i.e. 2027.

4.1.2. Pension fund projections
Long-term interest rates influence the projection of publicly funded pension funds and reserve
positions, in particular the expected rate of return on the stock of assets. Since the 2024 Ageing
Report, the reporting on pension fund assets and returns has been mandatory, whenever public pension
funds and reserve funds exist and are sizeable (17).
Considering the rising importance of pre-funded or partially funded public pension schemes in some
Member States, it is important to ensure consistent and comparable projections (i.e. based on common
assumptions) across all Member States of the asset position of these schemes and the related flows.
These projections contribute to the assessment of the sustainability of public pension schemes,
especially for Member States where these funds and reserves play a significant role. This is the case
for Finland, Luxembourg and Sweden.
The assumed rate of return for pension funds is
unchanged from previous reports. It is determined by
the long-term interest rate assumptions as described
in Section 4.1.1. Returns generated by the (public)
pension assets are generally reinvested in the funds
and need to be considered in the projections. Pension
funds might in reality achieve a return on equity that is
higher than the return on government bonds, though.
4.2.

INFLATION ASSUMPTIONS

The inflation methodology for the 2027 Ageing Report
is unchanged from previous reports. Beyond the
Commission 2026 spring forecast horizon in T+2
(2027), inflation is assumed to converge linearly to the
inflation targets of the central banks by T+5 (2030).
This target is 2% for euro area countries, Czechia,
Denmark and Sweden, 2.5% for Poland and Romania,
and 3% for Hungary.

5. SENSITIVITY TESTS AND ALTERNATIVE SCENARIOS
5.1.

INTRODUCTION

The baseline projections measure how population ageing influences economic and budgetary
developments in the long term. However, given the inherent uncertainty surrounding longrun projections, it is essential to conduct sensitivity tests to evaluate the responsiveness of
the projections to changes in the macroeconomic and demographic assumptions.
The sensitivity tests introduce a change to a specific variable. For each test, a uniform shock is applied
to all Member States. The sensitivity tests enrich the assessment of the impact of population ageing on
public spending. Combined with the baseline they allow identifying the key factors driving the
projection results and potential risks to the baseline. The sensitivity tests presented in section 5.2
include both unfavourable and favourable changes in the underlying assumptions.
In addition, specific policy scenarios are considered for each expenditure item to reflect the impact of
potential future policy changes. These scenarios reflect the impact of deviating from the constant
policy assumption under the baseline. Rather than attaching probabilities that the assumed policy is
effectively implemented, the policy scenarios provide a ‘what-if’ view to enrich the analysis. Section 5.3
provides the policy scenarios for the pension projections. The specific scenarios conducted for health
care, long-term care and education are presented in the respective chapters in Part II.
The projections for the sensitivity tests are constructed the same way as for the baseline: from
population to labour input to GDP growth. The Cohort Simulation Model and the production function are
adjusted in function of changes for each scenario (population, participation rate, TFP). However, in
these sensitivity exercises, changes in TFP and labour input do not lead to corresponding adjustments
in the capital stock, as is the case under the EUCAM methodology used for the baseline (see Chapter
3). The results should therefore be interpreted as partial sensitivity analyses rather than fully
consistent alternative macroeconomic projections.

5.2.

SENSITIVITY TESTS

The selection of sensitivity tests draws on the previous cycles. In general, the standard tests applied in
the 2024 Ageing Report were deemed appropriate for conducting a broad sensitivity analysis of the
baseline expenditure dynamics. Changes are therefore limited to the introduction of an additional
migration scenario. The assumptions are described in the following section and summarised in Table
I.5.1. Tables I.5.2 to I.5.9 summarise the macroeconomic projections for each sensitivity test. The
following sensitivity tests are conducted for all Member States( 18):

(18)

The five demographic sensitivity tests are based on demographic projections prepared by Eurostat around the baseline
demographic projections (EUROPOP2025) discussed in Chapter 1.

67

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

−

Higher life expectancy: the age-specific mortality rates are reduced to achieve an additional
increase in life expectancy at birth of two years by 2080 compared to the baseline assumptions.

− Lower fertility: fertility rates are assumed to be 20% lower from 2034 onwards. For 2025 and
2026, the rates coincide with the baseline assumptions. Between 2027–2034, intermediate values
are calculated by linear interpolation.
− Lower/higher migration: non-EU immigration flows are reduced/increased by 33% annually
compared with the baseline projection as of 2026.
− Migration – delayed convergence: net migration is assumed to converge more slowly because of
two elements: (i) the mid-term interpolation year is delayed from 2031 to 2040, extending countryspecific migration trends before converging towards common EU rates and levels; (ii) the workingage feedback mechanism is raised from 10% to 25%, thus increasing the immigration flows in
response to a decline in the working-age population (see Chapter 1). The AWG decided to add this
scenario for the 2027 round given the high uncertainty on future migration flows.
− Higher employment rate of older workers: through a reduction in the inactive population, the
employment rate of older workers (aged 55-74) is increased by 10 pps over the period 2031-2035
compared with the baseline and this difference is maintained until 2080.
− Lower/higher productivity growth: total factor productivity growth (TFP) is set at, respectively,
0.85 and 1.15 times the baseline projection once the output gap is closed, i.e. as of 2031.

5.3.

ALTERNATIVE PENSION SCENARIOS

In addition to the above sensitivity tests, three pension policy scenarios are conducted:
− Linking retirement ages to life expectancy: This scenario considers the adoption of a
mechanism to automatically update the statutory and early retirement ages in line with gains in life
expectancy at the age of 65 according to the EUROPOP2025 projections. As a result, effective
retirement ages rise gradually. To this end, the labour market exit ages as estimated through the
Cohort Simulation Model are adjusted. To account for the fact that people might have a higher risk
of becoming (partially) disabled at higher ages or would use alternative pathways to withdraw from
the labour force, the assumed increase in effective retirement ages equals about three quarters of
the estimated change in the statutory retirement age. For those countries where a full link between
the retirement age and the increase in life expectancy is currently legislated — and thus included in
the baseline — this scenario is not relevant. This is the case for Denmark, Estonia, Greece, Italy,
Cyprus and Slovakia. For the Netherlands, Portugal, Romania, Finland, Sweden and Norway, the
partial link is replaced by a full link.
− Constant retirement age: this scenario assumes that the main eligibility requirements (early and
statutory retirement age, career requirements) are unchanged over the projection period from
current conditions. To this end, the exit probabilities for people aged 51 and over in the CSM are
kept constant at their 2026 values. The scenario allows to isolate the expected impact of legislated,
though not yet implemented, reforms, e.g. links to life expectancy. In this sense, the scenario also
measures the budgetary impact of reversing reforms once their implementation date approaches.
− Constant benefit ratio: this scenario assumes measures are taken if, according to the baseline
projections, the earnings-related public pension benefit ratio decreases by more than 10% relative
to the base year at some point. In such case the benefit ratio is to be kept constant at this 10%
lower point for the rest of the projection period. In practice, ad hoc interventions may already occur
before the benefit ratio falls back to 90%. For countries that report private schemes, the change in
the total benefit ratio provides the benchmark, though the adjustment to keep the latter at 90% of
the base year level is done through the public benefit ratio. Since this scenario is conducted in
function of the baseline results, no separate macroeconomic assumptions are prepared.

Part II
Projection methodologies for age-related spending items

1. PENSIONS
1.1.

MAIN FEATURES OF THE PENSION PROJECTIONS

Despite the varying set-up of national health care, long-term care and education systems across
Member States, the European Commission and the AWG have developed common models to project
spending on these items. However, for pensions a different approach is taken since the diversity in
public pension systems is difficult to capture within a single framework. As a result, Member States use
their national models to prepare pension expenditure projections, incorporating the demographic and
macroeconomic assumptions described in Part I of this report.
On the one hand, this decentralised approach enables the integration of the specific institutional
features and legal settings of each country’s pension system as much as possible. On the other hand,
using separate models may reduce the comparability of the results, despite relying on common
methodologies and assumptions.
To ensure both a high quality and cross-country comparability, an extensive peer review is conducted
by the AWG members and the Commission. This process intends to: (i) verify whether the Member
States apply the agreed methodologies and the underlying demographic and macroeconomic
assumptions; and (ii) examine how Member States interpret the existing pension legislation. If deemed
necessary, the AWG asks the Member State to revise the projections.

1.2.

COVERAGE OF THE PENSION PROJECTIONS

The focus of the pension projection exercise is government expenditure on public pension schemes,
with variables for private schemes (occupational, individual mandatory or individual non-mandatory)
reported on a voluntary basis. Annex 3 provides an overview of which schemes exist in each country
and the extent to which they are covered in the projections.
Reporting is structured around the following items: gross pension expenditure by scheme and age;
taxes paid on pension benefits; benefit ratios and average replacement rates; the number of pensions
and pensioners by scheme, age and sex; contributions paid and the number of contributors; indexation
coefficients; the breakdown of new pension expenditure; and assets and reserves of pension funds.
Member States report administrative data for recent years on the number of new pensioners by sex
and public scheme as well as projections for new retirees by single age. Additionally, Member States
can provide, on a voluntary basis, details on special pension schemes. These are preferential schemes
that deviate from the standard regime in terms of eligibility criteria, benefit amount or contributions.
Such schemes should be included in the overall projections, which are exhaustive for public pension
expenditure. The scope and nature of special schemes are discussed in the country fiche accompanying
the updated projections for each country.

1.3.

DEFINITION OF THE VARIABLES

1.3.1. Reporting norms and input data
Member States provide annual projections up to 2080, with base year 2025, and report historical data
for the period 2000-2025. All expenditure figures are measured in current prices.
The GDP projections for each country over the period 2026-2080 are produced by the Commission by
means of a production function and the agreed assumptions (see Part I, Chapter 3).
Average wages are calculated as the ratio of total gross wages from national account data and
employed people (employees and self-employed) aged 15-74. The gross wage total without social

76

Part II
Projection methodologies for age-related spending items

security contributions is projected by multiplying GDP with the labour share and the ratio of gross
wages and salaries over compensation of employees (19). Dividing this series by the projections for
actual employment provides the average gross wage.
Projections on the economy-wide average wage at retirement are provided by the Member States. The
gross wage is net of employers’ contributions, while the net wage also excludes employees’
contributions and personal income taxation. These variables are required to calculate replacement
rates.
Member States provide historical data back to 2000 and discuss recent expenditure changes in the
country fiche. This should clarify the reasons behind: (i) notable changes in pension spending; (ii)
overall trends in pension spending in the recent past; and (iii) the possible implications of these
changes and trends for the projections.
The pension projections include the impact of pension reforms that will have entered legislation by the
cut-off date of the projections, i.e. 1 December 2026. To this end, in their country fiches Member
States provide a detailed discussion of the projections, including: (i) recently introduced reforms; (ii)
their implementation schedule; and (iii) how these reforms affect the projection results.

1.3.2. Variables: definitions and clarifications
Pension expenditure
Pension expenditure includes pensions and equivalent cash benefits granted for a long period (1 year
or more) for old age, early retirement, disability, survivors (widow(er)s and orphans), and other specific
purposes that should be considered as equivalents or substitutes for the mentioned types of pensions,
i.e. pensions or pension-like benefits granted because of a reduced work capacity or because of labour
market conditions.
Pension expenditure is projected according to the current legislation in force. Pensions are defined in a
broad sense: (i) earnings-related pensions; (ii) flat-rate pensions or basic pensions; (iii) means-tested
benefits that aim at providing a social minimum pension; and (iv) supplements that are part of the
pension benefit and that are granted for an indefinite period on the basis of fulfilling certain criteria
but which are not directly linked to the remuneration of costs (e.g. supplements to support the
purchase of home or health care services are not included). Pensions and benefits can be paid out from
specific schemes or directly from government budgets. Social assistance is included if it is functionally
equivalent to a minimum pension, thus representing a non-earnings-related minimum pension. Housing
subsidies are excluded and considered as other means-tested social transfers. Pension expenditure
should include pensions paid overseas, even if these people are not included in the demographic
projections.
Short-term disability benefits should be considered as sickness benefits, while prolonged unemployment benefits to older workers should be considered as unemployment benefits.
Pensions do not include social security contributions paid by pension schemes on behalf of their
pensioners to other social protection schemes, notably for health coverage.
Pension expenditure by age
Many countries have introduced pension reforms to lift the retirement age. To better understand the
impact of these reforms, Member States report public pension spending disaggregated by age
group: -54, 55-59, 60-64, 65-69, 70-74 and 75+. This breakdown increases transparency and
consistency between the projections for population, labour force and number of pensioners.

(19)

The labour share (ALCD0 in AMECO) and the ratio of gross wages and salaries (UWSH in AMECO) over compensation of
employees (UWCD in AMECO) are constant as of 2027 at the values from the Commission 2026 spring forecast.

77

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

New pension expenditure
To monitor reforms, Member States provide annual projections for spending on new benefits for each
of the pension schemes: (i) old-age and early pensions (earnings-related, flat-rate or basic pensions,
and minimum pensions), (ii) disability pensions, (iii) survivor pensions and (iv) other pensions (see
descriptions below).
Net pension expenditure
Net pension expenditure corresponds to gross pension expenditure minus taxes on pensions and
compulsory social security contributions paid by beneficiaries. Several EU countries treat pensions as
non-taxable income. In general, taxes are projected in a way that tax revenues as a share of pension
expenditure, the implicit average tax rate, stay constant over time.
Table II.1.1:

Typology of pension schemes in the Ageing Report

PUBLIC PENSIONS
Old-age and early pensions
- Basic and flat-rate pensions
- Earnings-related pensions
- Minimum pensions (non-contributory)

PRIVATE PENSIONS
Occupational schemes
Individual mandatory schemes
Individual voluntary schemes

Disability pensions
Survivor pensions
Other pension benefits

Source: European Commission, EPC.

Public pensions
Public schemes and other public pensions are statutory schemes that are provided by the general
government sector to insure against old age and other age-related risks. Typically it concerns universal
or quasi-universal social security schemes that are imposed, controlled and financed by government
units. Government employee schemes sometimes exist outside of the social security system (20).
The projections aim at covering in an exhaustive way those pension schemes that affect public
finances, in other words schemes that are considered to belong to the general government sector in
the national accounts system. Usually, there is a specific or general social security contribution to the
scheme, which is defined as part of total taxes in the national accounting system. However, the
scheme can also be financed, either partially or fully, through general taxation. Thus, ultimately, the
government bears the financial cost and risk attached to the scheme. The pensions provided by the
social security schemes can be either earnings-related, flat-rate or means-tested. In addition, public
pensions include pension benefits that are paid directly by the government budget or by another public
sector entity. Cash benefits equivalent to pensions, notably social assistance benefitting mainly older,
retired people, also fall within the Ageing Report pension definition.
Public pensions are split into four different categories: (i) old-age and early pensions, (ii) disability
pensions, (iii) survivor pensions and (iv) other types of pensions.
Old-age and early pensions
Old-age and early pensions include both earnings-related and non-earnings-related pensions. Earningsrelated pensions reflect all those pensions for which entitlements depend on earnings or contributions
to the scheme throughout one’s working career. Non-earnings-related pensions are often socialassistance benefits financed by taxes that nevertheless match the definition of pension expenditure.

(20)

78

See Eurostat (2020), Technical compilation guide for pension data in national accounts – 2020 edition.

Part II
Projection methodologies for age-related spending items

Three subcategories of old-age and early pensions are considered: basic pensions, earnings-related
pensions and non-contributory minimum pensions.
− Basic pensions (flat component) are projected separately from other earnings-related public
pensions for the countries with a flat pension component. This concerns pension benefits in flat
amounts allocated to beneficiaries meeting certain requirements, for instance residence or a
minimum number of contributory years.
− Earnings-related benefits other than basic pensions concern all pensions for which
entitlements depend on personal earnings or contributions to the old-age pension scheme. Old-age
and early pensions are considered as a single category given that in many countries no proper
distinction between the two can be made, for instance because the early retirement is built-in in the
old-age pension system or because the standard retirement age varies in function of career length.
Early pensions include – in addition to genuine (actuarial) early retirement schemes – other early
retirement schemes that are not disability pensions and are granted, primarily based on reduced
work capacity or labour market reasons, to a specific group at an age below the statutory
retirement age. This category also includes minimum pensions that are contributory based.
− Non-contributory minimum pensions and minimum income guarantees are projected
separately. This category includes all pension expenditure for which entitlement does not depend on
contribution requirements, e.g. means-tested minimum pensions. In line with the general definition
of pension expenditure, through this variable the projections include social assistance equivalent to
a minimum pension and targeted to older or retired people. If a non-contributory benefit is granted
because of disability or other reasons, this benefit is projected under ‘Disability pensions’ or ‘Other
pensions’.
Disability pensions
Expenditure related to disability does not distinguish between earnings- and non-earnings-related
disability pensions. In some countries disability benefits are part of the sickness insurance scheme
while in others they belong to the pension scheme (21). Disability pensions are generally transformed
into old-age pension benefits when beneficiaries reach the statutory retirement age.
Care allowances (benefits paid to disabled people who need frequent or constant assistance to cover
the extra costs of attendance) and integration support for people with disabilities (allowances paid
when having work adapted to a disability, e.g. a sheltered workshop) are not considered disability
pensions.
The country fiches provide a detailed description of the assumptions underlying the projection of
disability pension expenditure, including the assumed development of disability rates over the
projection period.
Survivor pensions
Survivor pensions, without any age limit, are covered by the projections. Such benefits include both
earnings-related pensions and flat-rate or similar means-tested minimum pensions. The country fiches
provide a detailed description of the assumptions behind the projection of survivor pension
expenditure.
Other pensions
All public earnings or non-earnings-related pensions not fitting in the three above categories should be
covered under ‘Other pensions’. Examples could be bonus pensions or pension-like benefits included in
the Ageing Report pension definition.

(21)

In general, disability pensions as defined above should be covered by the pension projections, even when they are
officially part of the sickness insurance scheme.

79

European Commission
2027 Ageing Report

Private pensions
Private pension schemes are covered on a voluntary basis in the projections. Distinction is made
between three types: (i) occupational private pensions, (ii) mandatory individual pensions and (iii)
voluntary individual pensions. Annex 3 provides an overview of the extent to which these schemes exist
in the Member States and whether the projections include them.
Occupational private pensions
Pensions provided by private occupational schemes are those that, rather than being statutory by law,
are linked to an employment relationship with the scheme provider. They are based on contractual
agreements between employers and employees, either at the company or sector/industry level. Private
pension funds, insurance companies or the sponsoring companies themselves run the schemes.
These schemes can be mandatory in the sense that, based on a nation- or industry-wide bargaining
agreement, employers are obliged to provide their employees with an occupational pension scheme. In
case there is auto-enrolment with the possibility for participants to opt-out afterwards, the schemes
are considered ‘quasi’ mandatory. Participation of an individual can also remain voluntary. Private
occupational schemes can be equivalent to statutory earnings-related pension schemes or
complementary to them. Of particular importance are the occupational schemes that play a role
equivalent to social security schemes in the overall pension provision. The real rate of return on private
funded pensions reflects the long-term interest rate assumptions (see Part I, Chapter 4).
Private individual pensions
In this case, access to the scheme does not require an employment relationship, even though in some
cases the contribution is based on the wage. Insured persons have the ownership of pension assets so
that they both enjoy the rewards and bear the risks associated to the assets’ valuation. The insurance
contract specifies a contribution schedule in exchange for which benefits will be paid when the
member reaches a specific age, usually the statutory or earliest retirement age. The scheme provider
administers the scheme by managing the pension assets through a separate account on behalf of its
member.
Private individual pension schemes are mostly non-mandatory, though some countries have introduced
mandatory schemes (see Annex 3).
Mandatory private individual pensions
Mandatory private pension schemes are similar to public schemes in that they are compulsory.
Transactions occur between the individual and the insurance provider. Transactions are not recorded as
government revenues or government expenditure and, therefore, do not have an impact on the
government balance. This category of private benefits mostly concerns pensions that were formerly
part of the social security scheme but that have been switched to funded private schemes.
In some cases, these pension schemes benefit from government guarantees. Nevertheless, such a
guarantee is a contingent liability by nature, and these liabilities are not considered as economic
transactions until they materialise. Thus, a government guarantee is not an adequate condition to
classify such schemes as social security schemes.
Non-mandatory individual private pensions
Non-mandatory private pensions are based on individual insurance contracts between the individual
and the private pension scheme provider, usually an insurance company or a pension fund. They
include schemes for which membership is not required by law and is independent of any employment
link (even if members are mostly employed people). However, in some cases employers or the State
may contribute to the plan. Such schemes may also be joined through membership of an association.
The main difficulty in analysing individual saving provisions stems from the fact that it is hard to
distinguish among different types of savings those that are clearly for retirement purposes. Part of the
savings that are not specifically labelled as pension savings may be used for retirement purposes,

80

Part II
Projection methodologies for age-related spending items

whereas part of the savings in retirement schemes may in practice not be converted into a periodic
retirement income, e.g. in case they result in a one-off lump-sum pay-out or there are early withdrawal
possibilities. The extent to which these schemes are effectively retirement savings depends on the
attached conditions. Conditions could be, for instance, tax deductions on the condition that savings are
converted into a rent rather than paid out as a lump sum, or the minimum age at which people can
access their savings.

1.3.3. Benefit ratio and replacement rate at retirement
The benefit ratio and the replacement rate help interpret changes in average pension benefits.
Benefit ratio
The benefit ratio is the average pension benefit over the average wage. If private pensions are
reported, also a total benefit ratio is available.
The benefit ratio projections capture several dynamics. First, they reflect the increase in average
pensions due to indexation rules, the maturity of the system (especially in case schemes are being
phased in or out), rising contribution periods, as well as possible changes in the pension formula.
Second, it reflects the changes in average wages that result from the labour productivity assumptions.
Third, it captures the changes in the structure of the respective population groups, namely the share of
pensioners and workers over the projection period.
Replacement rate at retirement
The average replacement rate at retirement is the ratio of the first average pension benefit of new
retirees in a given year over the average wage at retirement. The latter usually differs from the
average wage unless a flat wage profile over the entire working career is assumed in the projection
exercise. For public pension schemes, the average replacement rate reflects only earnings-related
pensions, including the flat component.
The replacement rate provides insights in the decrease in income that results from moving from
employment into retirement. The size of this decline is influenced, for example, by the contribution
period, valorisation rules, bonuses/penalties in case of delayed/early retirement, and sustainability
factors.
Both gross and net replacement rates are calculated. The gross variant relates the gross average new
pension benefit to the gross wage at retirement (excluding employers’ contributions), while the net
variant relates the net average new pension benefit (excluding taxes and mandatory social
contributions if applicable) to the average net wage at retirement (excluding all contributions and
personal income taxes).
